#6881. Birkenstock Clogs Are Easier to Dress Up Than You Think

Every item on this page was chosen by an ELLE editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Birkenstock clogs have never really gone out of style, but when skinny jeans were replaced with wide-leg and bootcut jeans, they firmly reclaimed their place on the shoe rack. Now that sneakers go way more places than the gym and the grocery store, you should give your Birkenstocks another glance and see your Bostons as a fashion opportunity.

Plus, Birkenstocks have gone far beyond the taupe Boston—the label has made clog variations like the Mary Jane-inspired Maria and modern Loma. Or if you’re still partial to the Boston, you can get the clog style in a variety of cool colors, like Oyster and Thyme, and variations, like big buckles or braided straps.

If you’re not sure how you can transition your Bostons from your throw-on, afterthought shoe, we’re here to help. Below, I’ve compiled some outfit inspiration for you and the pieces you need to re-create it.

Birkenstocks at the office? Absolutely—they are clogs after all, and people wear sneakers to the office all the time. Why wear those flats that give you blisters when your Bostons are right there? Pair them with a coordinating trench and white wide-leg jeans (because we haven’t been following that archaic white-after-Labor-Day rule all fall).

Bernardo Belted Stretch Cotton Trench Coat

Even supermodel and Guest In Residence founder Gigi Hadid can’t resist a comfy pair of Birks. For a perfectly executed, effortless, and casual look, pair your Bostons with a graphic tee and bootcut jeans. Create some visual interest by tying your cardigan or sweatshirt like Hadid, instead of simply around your neck. Now that Bostons are everywhere again, opt for a color like Oyster to set yourself apart from the Taupe pack.

Birkenstocks with a matching sweater set? Hear me out—I’m going to cite stylist Allison Bornstein’s “wrong shoe theory.” It’s been around for a while, but wearing a historically “wrong” shoe with an outfit is often so, so right. You probably have never thought about pairing your Birkenstocks with a skirt, but if you go monochrome, like I’m suggesting, it creates a visually interesting outfit. I suggest opting for Birkenstock’s Maria, a Mary Jane-esque variation on the Boston, if you want a sleeker silhouette.

Everyone has a comfy errands uniform—myself included—but instead of slipping on those track pants or leggings with your Birks for the umpteenth time, maybe elevate your look with some silk pants and a designer suede tote or deviate from the Birkenstock Boston. I recommend checking out the Loma, a new wrap-style clog silhouette from the brand, a modern and playful take on the classic Boston.

Take a page out of this off-duty model’s playbook. Don’t be afraid to mix neutral tones, like cream, black, and gray. The mélange of textures—the suede clogs, colored denim, heather polo, and quilted bag—creates an effortlessly cozy look. Trust me, you’ll be wearing this on repeat on the weekends this fall and winter.

Tory Burch Kira Diamond Quilted Leather Tote

#6880. Everything to Know About Nicholas Hoult’s Wife, Bryana Holly

They just made their red carpet debut at the premiere of Nosferatu.

Nicholas Hoult’s latest flick How to Rob a Bank is about to hit theaters, which means the actor is in the thick of a press tour for the project and making rounds on red carpets.

He made a stop at Jake Shane’s podcast, Therapuss, during his press tour on November 11 and made rare comments about his wife—yes, wife—Bryana Holly.

I know what you’re thinking: How long has it been since Nicholas Hoult and Jennifer Lawrence called it quits, and when did he start dating Bryana? Ahead, find everything we know about Nicholas Hoult’s wife, Bryana Holly.

Bryana is signed to Wilhemina Models and occasionally poses for lingerie, swimsuit, and clothing brands. She also starred in Hoodie Allen’s music video for “Movie” in 2015:

If Bryana looks a little familiar, you may recognize her from her past relationships. The model was romantically linked to Brody Jenner in 2013 before she dated 5 Seconds of Summer drummer Ashton Irwin from 2015 to 2016.

The pair has been together since 2017, and in 2024, Nicholas confirmed they secretly tied the knot.

At the Governors Awards in November 2024, Nicholas called Bryana his “wife” while talking to E! News. “Afterwards, I remember I had my wife’s nails imprinted on my hand,” he said of her reaction to Nosferatu.

A few days later, he confirmed they were married to People, saying, “I feel like it’s fairly clear from my comments.”

Remember that Therapuss interview I mentioned earlier? Well, Nicholas shared the story of how he met his wife in 2016.

“I was shooting a movie,” he said during his convo with Jake. “We were kind of filming and shooting a Halloween party at the same time. And then we had actually a really fun…it was kind of an overcommitment first date.”

He shared that he and Bryana started chatting about their music taste and decided to go to the Desert Trip music festival (which he claimed is “affectionately known” as “Oldchella). They apparently bonded over listening to artists like The Rolling Stones, Paul McCartney, Bob Dylan, and The Who.

“We didn't really know each other, but we both wanted to go,” he explained. “We went to dinner once before just to be like, ‘Let’s make sure neither of us are complete lunatics.’”

Nicholas brought Bryana as his date to the Nosferatu premiere in L.A. back in 2024. And, if you were wondering, this marks their first red carpet appearance after being together for seven years.

Bryana and Nicholas welcomed their first child, Joaquin, in 2018. He’s been seen out and about with the Skins alum and has even made a rare appearance on Bryana’s Instagram (which is now private, womp).

The couple then quietly welcomed their second child in 2022, and according to Nicholas’ How to Rob a Bank co-star, Pete Davidson, they also gave birth to twins at some point.

“He’s a great dad, father of four,” Pete told Entertainment Tonight. “Four under what? 7 [years old] ... which is nuts.”

He also praised Nicholas for how much he catered to his family while filming.

“Elsie was pregnant while we were filming and he just had his twins,” Pete continued. “He would fly back every weekend to go see his family, and that was very inspiring and awesome.”

#6879. How to Read the Dune Book Series in Order

Our foolproof guide to getting caught up before Dune: Part Three hits theaters.

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

So you're fired up about Dune: Part Three? The trilogy-capper is shaping up to be one of the biggest movies of the year, costarring just about every A-lister in Hollywood, including Timothée Chalamet, Zendaya, Robert Pattinson, and Florence Pugh. If you want to dive into the world of Frank Herbert's beloved novels before the film’s release on December 18, congratulations! You’ve come to the right place.

Dune is the world's best-selling science fiction novel. It's also considered one of the best books of all time. But whether you've dabbled in Dune lore before or you're completely new to the wild world of Arrakis, there's something for everyone in this Titanic-sized series about power, violence, and fate. You've got an exciting literary journey ahead.

Published in 1965, the book's massive success inspired Herbert to write several sequels, which brought the total number of novels in the series up to six when he died in 1986. The first Dune novel was split in half to cover both of Denis Villeneuve’s films so far, and the third novel, Dune: Messiah, will be adapted in Dune: Part Three. From there? Well, your guess is as good as mine.

Not only did Herbert write four more Dune novels, but his son Brian Herbert continued to write a little under two dozen more books in the Dune franchise following his father’s death. He teamed up with sci-fi writer Kevin J. Anderson to coauthor a trilogy of Dune prequels and then several spin-offs thereafter that filled in the gaps in Dune’s history.

But what's the right way to start your Dune journey? Well, there's no right answer. Some argue that the books should be read in the order of their fictive timeline, while others argue that they should be read in the order of publication.

Here's our advice: Read Frank Herbert's six novels first, then dabble in the spin-offs and prequels however you like. Given that many are grouped into smaller series within the larger story, you can sample bits and pieces of the universe. That's the beauty of Dune—it's a detailed series that rewards completionism, but the entry points are numerous.

Read on for a full breakdown of the books, listed here in the order in which they were published. Happy reading, spiceheads!—Josh Rosenberg

Dune is set far in the future, in an intergalactic feudal society where powerful noble houses fight for control over resources, armies, and planetary power. House Atreides is ordered to take control of Arrakis, a barren desert planet with a brutal climate. Arrakis is the only place to mine melange, a natural resource that produces a psychoactive drug called spice, which allows human beings to unlock their minds. On Arrakis, House Atreides is betrayed by its rival House Harkonnen, which sets off a battle over the valuable planet. The planet itself is inhabited by giant sandworms and a native population known as Fremen, who, over the generations, have learned to survive with water as their most precious resource and currency. When House Atreides scion Paul is targeted as a potential messiah to lead the planet—and galaxy—toward a new era, an epic story of war, betrayal, and mysticism unfolds. Cinephiles, this one is a must: Denis Villeneuve's Dune and Dune: Part Two span this book.

In Herbert's first sequel, Paul Atreides, now known as Muad'Dib, rules the known universe as the most powerful emperor of all time. Worshipped as a messiah by the people of Arrakis, Paul faces enmity from the warring political houses under his control. Is any one ruler meant to have such absolute power? In Dune Messiah, Herbert unmakes his previous novel, all while warning, "No more terrible disaster could befall your people than for them to fall into the hands of a hero." Film fans, don't miss this one—you'll want to be caught up when Villeneuve's Dune Part Three lands in theaters on December 19, 2026.

Children of Dune picks up with Leto and Ghanima Atreides, the twin children of Paul Atreides, nine years after their father's mysterious disappearance into the wastelands of Arrakis. The twins' prophetic abilities are coveted by their manipulative aunt Alia, who rules the Empire, but these two young prophets refuse to be anyone's pawns.

Thirty-five hundred years after the events of Children of Dune, the once-desert planet of Arrakis is now a lush paradise, and Leto Atreides sits on the throne. Millennia ago, Leto merged with a sandworm to grant himself immortality, but the cost to his humanity has been enormous. Can a rebellion led by Siona, a rival relative, unseat this fearsome despot?

Leto Atreides is dead, Arrakis (now called Rakis) is once again a desert wasteland, and the Empire has fallen into ruin. A young girl named Sheeana seems to fulfill a prophecy foretold by the late God Emperor, sending religious fervor through the galaxy. Is Sheeana destined to return the Empire to its former glory?

In Frank Herbert's final Dune novel, Arrakis has been destroyed, and the fate of the Empire rests in the hands of a mysterious matriarchal order known as the Bene Gesserit. On the planet Chapterhouse, the sisters are breeding sandworms and seeking to control spice production, with the goal of remaking the galaxy for a brighter future.

Brian Herbert (Frank Herbert’s son) and Kevin J. Anderson's Prelude to Dune trilogy begins with this story of the generation before the one in Dune—namely, that of Leto Atreides, father of Paul. In House Atreides, we see how Leto's rivalries and relationships sowed the catalyzing events of Dune.

In House Harkonnen, Leto Atreides's longtime rival, the Baron Vladimir Harkonnen, comes into view. We also meet Abulurd Rabban, brother and foil to the baron. Turns out, there are good people in House Harkonnen—who knew?

In House Corrino, Herbert and Anderson conclude their prequel series, bringing the story up to the climactic events set to unfold in Dune. This tapestry of politics, warmongering, and spice battles ends with the birth of Paul Atreides, preparing us for the saga we know and love (and now have already read).

Working from Frank Herbert's notes for a "Dune 7," his son and Kevin J. Anderson again expanded the series in Legends of Dune, a new trilogy. The first installment, The Butlerian Jihad, digs into an event Herbert often referred to but never captured at scale: the long-ago war in which humans fought for their freedom from "the thinking machines." Set 10,000 years before Dune, this volume sees the familiar chess pieces come into view.

The Legends of Dune trilogy continues with The Machine Crusade, set two decades after The Butlerian Jihad. The thinking machines fight back, refusing to go quietly into that good night. Meanwhile, on Arrakis, a band of outlaws take their first steps to becoming the Fremen, a race of people that OG Dune fans know and love.

Check out that sandworm on the cover! The Legends of Dune trilogy concludes with The Battle of Corrin, which tees up a final apocalyptic showdown between humans and robots. Fans of Dune know how this one ends, but it sure is fun to see how Herbert and Anderson get there.

Dune Sequels, a two-volume spin-off series, concludes the storyline from Herbert's six original novels, with insight from a long-lost outline found hidden in one of the author's safety deposit boxes. In Hunters of Dune, we pick up with the escaping fugitives last seen at the end of Chapterhouse: Dune as they strengthen their powers and fight for the future of the human race.

Herbert and Anderson answer more burning questions in this second volume of the Dune Sequels series—namely, the future of Arrakis and the outcome of the war between man and machine.

Set between Dune and Dune Messiah, the Heroes of Dune spin-off series opens with a tale of Paul Atreides. Dune ends with Paul ruling Arrakis, while Dune Messiah opens with him ruling the galaxy. Just how did he gain control of the Empire? Read Paul of Dune to find out.

Heroes of Dune continues with this second and final installment, set after the events of Dune Messiah. The Winds of Dune picks up after Paul Atreides's disappearance into the Arrakis desert, leaving the Empire in crisis and the line of succession in question. Who will hold everything together?

The Great Schools of Dune trilogy opens with Sisterhood of Dune, set almost a century after the game-changing Battle of Corrin. With the thinking machines destroyed, political and religious movements rise, setting the stage for an epic conflict between reason and faith. You'll want to read this one to understand more about HBO's Dune: Prophecy series.

In Book Two of the Schools of Dune Trilogy, the thinking machines are gone, but a new school opens to teach humans the efficient techniques of those machines. The Butlerian jihadists staunchly oppose any machinist way of life, however, and pick a dangerous fight with the Mentat School. What ensues is a showdown for humanity's future, with the risk of a potential dark age.

Want to learn even more about the Bene Gesserit sisterhood from Herbert's original novels? Then Navigators of Dune is the book for you. In this third and final volume of the Great Schools of Dune trilogy, we learn about the origins of the Bene Gesserit and see their secretive way of life develop.

The latest trilogy from Herbert and Anderson, The Caladan Trilogy, begins with The Duke of Caladan, a prequel about the life of Leto Atreides. Just how did the ruler of a quiet planet become such a power player in a galactically fateful story? If that's the question on your mind, this is the book you ought to hit next.

Herbert and Anderson take it all the way back to OG Dune in this prequel about Lady Jessica, mother of Paul Atreides. In The Lady of Caladan, Lady Jessica reckons with her choice to betray the Bene Gesserit, her ancient maternal order, while destiny converges around her husband and son.

Have you read everything up to this point about Paul Atreides but you still can't get enough of the guy? We've got a book for you. The Caladan Trilogy concludes with The Heir of Caladan, a deep dive into the young heir's metamorphosis from teenager to messiah.

Set two years before the events of Dune, Princess of Dune delves into the lives of the two most important women in Paul Atreides's life: his wife, Princess Irulan, and his true love, Chani. Though these characters are often at odds in their separate quests to influence Paul, Princess of Dune draws striking parallels between these brave, ambitious, and embattled young women. Through their expanded backstory, one comes to see the events of Dune in a new light.

Superfans will love this collection of novellas, each one taking place in an under-explored corner of the Dune-verse. Just what was Gurney Halleck up to during his lost years working undercover as an Arrakis smuggler? What's it like to be one of the fearsome Sardaukar? How did Shadout Mapes go from a Fremen freedom fighter to an imperial housekeeper? If you've ever asked these questions, you'll find your answers in Sands of Dune.

The Road to Dune contains short stories, previously unpublished works, and correspondence from the late Frank Herbert that all led to the creation of Dune. Plus, you can read the novella “Spice Planet,” based on a detailed outline left by Frank Herbert.

#6877. What Does the Winner of ‘Dancing With the Stars’ Get, Exactly?

It’s a very different situation for the pro vs. the celeb.

Dancing With the Stars is wrapping up season 35 and someone is about to walk away with a glittery mirrorball trophy and a bunch of money. Actually several someones are going to walk away with a bunch of money, because Dancing With the Stars shares the love between multiple contestants when it comes to winnings. If you have questions, we have answers, so let’s get to it.

Nope. The winner of Dancing With the Stars doesn’t get extra money for winning—they just get the Mirrorball Trophy. However, they do get financially compensated for their time on the show—within an established payment structure.

The cast makes money for each week they remain on the show. According to Variety, everyone takes home $125,000 for the rehearsal period, as well as the first two weeks DWTS airs—meaning, even if you’re voted off you still get paid. The remaining celebs then get bonuses each week they continue.

A while back, there were reports (via Variety) that contestants got an additional $10,000 for weeks three and four, an additional $20,000 for weeks five and six, an additional $30,000 for weeks seven and eight, and then another $50,000 for weeks nine and ten.

This means that all the final celebs who make it to the Dancing With the Stars finale earn $295,000, regardless of whether or not they win.

Pros earn a per-episode salary. We have a full breakdown this week, but according to Us Weekly the highest-paid dancers (aka the pros who have been around for the longest) make around $5,000 an episode.

But there’s no extra baked-in “prize” for the pros if they secure a win, though there is a bonus. Jenna Johnson explained how it works to The Morning After podcast, saying “Whoever makes it to the finale, you all get paid up until then. And you do get a nice bonus. If you win, it’s not like you get a million dollars. You’re just getting a cute trophy together.”

Jenna also confirmed that pros who are voted out earlier are “guaranteed until a certain amount of weeks” pay. Which is something Brandon Armstrong’s wife, Brylee, confirmed on TikTok last year, saying “Brandon is still very much a pro on the show. Just because his partner was eliminated doesn’t mean that he just gets the boot and he’s gotta pack his bags and go all the way back to Utah.” Brylee added that “Dancing with the Stars takes care of their people, so no need to worry.”

#6874. Is The Rings of Power Still Worth Watching?

As The Lord of the Rings prequel enters its third of five planned seasons, let’s admit that Jeff Bezos spent a little too much money making this a reality.

When I was a kid, I was obsessed with something called Mystic Knights of Tir Na Nog. If you weren’t a first-grader in 1998, it was a Fox Kids Saturday morning series about four heroes in medieval Ireland who morphed into armored knights to battle dragons and evil witches. It was Power Rangers by way of Celtic mythology, and it kicked ass. I was a wee bit too young for Hercules and Xena then, and Mystic Knights made sword-and-sorcery stories accessible to my Saturday morning-addled brain. Two years after it went off the air, The Lord of the Rings: The Fellowship of the Ring stormed into theaters.

However unfair, it’s through that spectrum of two extreme ends that I see fantasy television like The Lord of the Rings: The Rings of Power. I have a standard through which I see this genre in this particular medium. On the big screen, fantasy can feel endless, its scope stretching beyond the horizon. On the small screen, you can feel the ceiling over everyone’s heads. Even Game of Thrones had a coffee cup slip through the veil. Which is why, despite the distant relation to Peter Jackson’s immaculate movies, The Rings of Power is quintessential fantasy TV on par with Mystic Knights. That’s not a bad thing—it’s just perspective.

We’re four years and three seasons (lol) into The Rings of Power’s planned five seasons on Prime Video. After all this time, it still hasn’t won over skeptics. There are long and boring reasons why Tolkien faithful have been averse to the prequel show. They have complaints about pacing, plotting, creative liberties from Tolkien’s textbooks of lore, and general execution. Regrettably, they more often than not get mixed up with old-fashioned racism and misogyny. (Valar forbid a dwarf be a Black woman.) But sometimes the worst people you know have a point: The Rings of Power has been an underwhelming experience. Despite its strong start in season 1, the show simply hasn’t escaped the long, long shadow of Jackson’s cinematic trilogy. (Even Jackson never lived up to his own standards. See: The Hobbit.)

Amid the show’s large ensemble, the series bet many of its chips on Charlie Vickers as Sauron. Though handsome, Vickers’ lack of charisma, middling scriptwriting for his role, and a severe lack of sexual tension with co-star Morfydd Clark (as Galadriel) have sadly made Rings of Power somnambulant. Now in season 3, the chance to course-correct is long past, and the rigor mortis of its missteps have now settled in.

Season 1 had a strong start. Sauron is a hottie, and his early relationship to Galadriel carried with it an air of intrigue and yearning until his mask came off. Maybe there could have been a whole show about their forbidden attraction. (Some fans have already coined “Saurondriel.”) The good girl/bad boy pairing is a tried and true tradition, with a subset of the Star Wars fandom possessing a bizarre obsession with Daisy Ridley’s Rey getting it on with Adam Driver’s Kylo Ren. Who are any of us to deny ourselves this pleasure?

But season 2 didn’t seize on nor improve what was its grace. Their characters were kept separate until the finale, where they reunited on the battlefield. It’s a good scene, to be fair, it’s just maddening that what could have been the whole show was just five minutes, including a bloodless sword fight.

Now here we are in season 3, and it’s more of the same. All of the characters are on a journey across Middle-earth, the story is moving along at a steady, if at times meandering pace, Sauron is quite literally plotting everything from his high tower, and there is a clear endpoint at the end of it all: the forging of the One Ring. As of this writing, the season’s four episodes (available to stream now) suggest we’ll get there in due time. There have been some big-name casualties already, and some impressive set pieces that actually feel like things are intensifying at the right temperatures.

Still, this is Lord of the Rings we’re talking about here. For all the lore and imagination Tolkien spilled onto his pages, his was a story still mired in the age-old tradition of good versus evil. That is Rings of Power too, where even morality comes in shades of stark black and white (mostly) and just enough polished VFX and production design to sell the immersion. One could ask, “What more do you want?” Somehow, it’s still not really enough. Even now, there is still too little energy, too little thrust, too little verve—and yes, still too little sex appeal—to warrant undivided attention. The Rings of Power is mighty pretty to look at on a big 50-inch LED screen. (I’m particularly engrossed anytime we follow Daniel Weyman’s Gandalf, who is always strolling through the most picturesque backdrops.) But it’s also far too easy to get distracted by more exciting things, like your laundry machine timer going off. In other words: The Rings of Power is a television show.

For all the money Jeff Bezos spent on making Rings of Power a reality, it’s still just TV. It’s maybe a cruel, cosmic, and frankly righteous joke that the billionaire’s very clear marching orders to his team—“I want my Game of Thrones,” is how he was quoted—have resulted in precisely that. A fantasy television series. Blame the streaming era for pigeonholing everything of this caliber into a blockbuster with lofty expectations. Not all TV should be appointment viewing. Sometimes a TV show can and should just be what it is: Mystic Knights, with a few extra zeroes.

#6873. ‘Rivals’ Season 2’s Shocking Tony Baddingham Scene, Explained—And What Happens Next

David Tennant’s ruthless TV boss has officially reached breaking point.

Warning: Major spoilers for Rivals season 2, part 2 ahead.

Just when we thought things couldn't get any more dramatic in Rutshire, Rivals has delivered one of its most shocking moments yet. The second half of season 2 has finally landed on Disney+ and Hulu, picking up in the aftermath of Lady Monica Baddingham's (Claire Rushbrook) devastating death. And while her husband Tony (David Tennant) is struggling to come to terms with losing his wife, his grief is taking a seriously disturbing turn.

Because, in a scene that nobody saw coming, the ruthless Corinium Television boss takes a rifle to Monica's beloved peacocks. Yes, you read that correctly.

It's a particularly brutal moment, even by Tony's standards, and one that reveals just how far he's spiraling following his wife's death.

But what exactly pushed him to breaking point? And what does it mean for the rest of the season?

When we last visited Rutshire, Monica had been killed in a car accident after discovering that Tony was having an affair with Maud O'Hara (Victoria Smurfit).

The latest episodes pick up in the aftermath of her death, with Tony struggling to process his grief while facing the consequences of his infidelity.

While the TV boss has never exactly been known for his kindness, the scene takes his cruelty to another level.

The peacocks are a reminder of Monica and the life they shared, making his decision to kill them feel particularly vicious.

Rather than dealing with the pain of losing his wife, Tony appears to direct his anger toward something she loved.

Speaking to Deadline about the latest episodes, David and executive producer Dominic Treadwell-Collins discussed Tony's increasingly destructive behavior following Monica's death.

He told the outlet: "To lose your life partner is devastating. Even for someone who’s not always the most morally engaged human, I think it’s bewildering and it’s distressing for him. He’s rudderless without her, so I think it’s quite a plausible reaction.

"He’s a man who doesn’t have a particularly well-oiled path to his own emotions, so I think the fact that such a traumatic moment should end with some kind of violent exorcism makes perfect sense. I think that’s who Tony is, unfortunately."

For someone who has spent much of the series manipulating those around him, Tony suddenly finds himself unable to control the one thing that matters most: his own life.

Of course, there's also the small matter of his affair with Maud, which continues to complicate an already messy situation.

If you were hoping Monica's death might inspire Tony to turn over a new leaf, we wouldn't hold our breath.

Despite everything that's happened, Tony remains determined to maintain his grip on Corinium Television, with his bitter rivalry against Declan O'Hara (Aidan Turner) showing no signs of slowing down.

And things are about to get even more complicated now that Declan has discovered the truth about Tony's affair with Maud.

The revelation leads to an explosive confrontation during the Bonfire Night celebrations, leaving Declan and Maud's marriage hanging in the balance.

Meanwhile, Tony's relationship with his son Archie remains fractured, and his increasingly unpredictable behavior raises questions about just how far he's prepared to go to protect his position.

With the battle for the television franchise reaching a critical point, Tony could find himself facing consequences both professionally and personally.

And given what we've just witnessed, we're slightly terrified to see what he does next.

#6864. From Chocolate Chrome to Plaid Prints, These Are the Chicest Thanksgiving Nail Ideas to Try This Year

Your fingertips also deserve to be dressed up for the feast.

We may earn commission from links on this page, but we only recommend products we love. Promise.

We are officially in the thick of November, which means festive nail art is now on the horizon. And with Thanksgiving just a few weeks away, there's no better time to lean into a playful fall manicure than at your next nail appointment.

I rarely need an excuse to get a fresh set. Still, Thanksgiving feels like a particularly good one to experiment with autumnal designs and shades that are perfectly on-theme for the big feast. Between family dinners and Friendsgiving celebrations, there are plenty of opportunities to show off your nails. “The holiday season is the perfect excuse to embrace maximalist nail art,” says celebrity nail artist Max Estrada. “If your nails want to dress up for dinner, let them.”

Ahead, I chatted with nail artists about their favorite Thanksgiving nail ideas, along with polish recommendations to help you recreate a turkey-day-ready look.

“Thanksgiving is all about family gatherings, cozy evenings, and the warmth of the fall season,” says celebrity nail artist Vikki Swan. “I gravitate toward earthy tones and designs that reflect this atmosphere, so I especially love plaid patterns.” You can choose classic stripes or argyle prints, and go for an all over pattern or a subtle French tip. It’s quite literally the manicure equivalent of your favorite knit sweater.

Let the changing colors of fall foliage be the inspo behind your next manicure. “I’m loving gold leaf and metallic foils for Thanksgiving,” says Estrada. “The irregular placement of the leafing is part of the beauty and you don’t need every nail to look identical.” You’ll be sure to have the most on-theme manicure at the holiday table.

“I love a classic French manicure with a seasonal twist, such as chocolate brown, caramel, or burgundy tips,” says Swan. Start off with a milky or nude base, and simply choose your favorite fall hue for the tip of the nail.

Animal prints in the brown hue family, like tiger, cheetah, and fawn, are an ideal pattern to lean into for November. They won’t feel stale days after your Thanksgiving feast is over, either. There are plenty of print variations to play with, but I especially love the look of a milky base with spots or stripes layered on top in the shape of a French tip.

Both Estrada and Swan love a warm brown polish this time of year, “paired with a subtle pearl chrome finish provides beautiful depth and a soft shimmer to the nails,” Swan says. “Cat-eye effects are another big part of the conversation,” Estrada adds. “They give those rich colors movement and dimension, so the manicure becomes interesting through the finish itself.” You can’t go wrong with OPI’s Cliffside Karaoke or Zoya’s Thatcher.

Why settle on one fall hue when you can wear a different shade on every finger? “Using different shades from the same color family across the nails and transitioning from lighter to deeper tones, is a simple yet elegant way to capture the fall mood without intricate nail art,” says Swan. Just choose a few of your favorite polishes, and they don’t need to match perfectly, either.

Jasmine Hyman is the assistant beauty editor at Cosmo who covers all things nails—from the latest design trends to new polish launches. For this story, she consulted celebrity nail artists for their favorite Thanksgiving nail ideas.

#6858. New Industry Alliance to Work Toward Common Security Model for AI Agents

Okta has announced a coalition bringing together major cloud, security, data and application vendors to create a shared architecture for securing AI agents across enterprise environments.

The 12 founding members of the new Blueprint Alliance are AWS, CrowdStrike, Databricks, Docker, Google Cloud, Lovable, Okta, Proofpoint, Salesforce, ServiceNow, Wiz and Zscaler. GE Appliances and World Central Kitchen are participating as strategic advisors.

The effort expands an earlier Okta framework into an open, multi-vendor reference architecture intended to address a basic problem with enterprise AI agents: An agent can be created in one system, authenticated through another, access tools and data from several others, and be monitored by yet another security platform.

The Alliance is focused less on how agents are built and more on how organizations can govern them consistently across vendor boundaries.

The Blueprint Alliance architecture organizes agent security around four questions: Where are my agents? What can they do? What are they doing? How do I respond?

Those questions translate into several practical areas:

The reference architecture also covers development-related systems. Its whitepaper includes agent frameworks, code repositories, CI/CD environments, code assistants, tool registries, Model Context Protocol servers, and command-line tools among the components that may need to be discovered or governed.

That makes the initiative relevant to development teams deploying agents that interact with internal APIs, MCP tools, databases, SaaS applications, or other agents. The architecture calls for those connections and permissions to remain visible and controllable as agents move from development into production.

A major part of the Alliance's work will be testing how security information and controls can move between products from different vendors.

The initial architecture references several existing standards and protocols, including MCP for agent-to-tool interactions, the Open Cybersecurity Schema Framework for security telemetry, and the Shared Signals Framework and Continuous Access Evaluation Profile for exchanging risk information.

The goal is for one part of an enterprise stack to be able to detect a risk condition and pass that information to another control point that can act on it. Depending on the implementation, that could include revoking access or containing an agent whose behavior has changed.

The Alliance says members will build and test reference integrations and publish results as interoperability work progresses. Specific cross-vendor implementations and performance results were not documented in the launch announcement.

Founding member Lovable also published a developer-oriented explanation of its participation, describing organizations that may have internally built agents, agents imported through SaaS platforms and locally running agents that all need to be inventoried and governed under a common model.

The Alliance's whitepaper recommends that organizations begin with foundational capabilities such as unified logging, telemetry, and agent discovery rather than attempting to deploy every control at once.

The architecture is available for community use, and the Alliance says it will continue evolving as members test integrations and additional participants become involved. Timelines for individual vendor integrations have not been published.

For more information, check out the Okta announcement here.

David Ramel is an editor and writer at Converge 360.

Unauthorized artificial intelligence tools and agents are creating security risks for organizations as AI adoption increases, according to cybersecurity company Sophos.

#6856. Meet the Cast and Characters of ‘The Good Daughter’

Excited to watch The Good Daughter on Peacock, but didn’t read the book by Karin Slaughter that it’s based on? Here’s everything you need to know about the cast and characters of The Good Daughter, without giving anything away. Well, maybe one thing. There’s a character who only shows up in one timeline for a reason. You might guess why, but I’m going to spoil it later just so we’re all on the same page.

First of all, no... this series is not connected to The Good Wife, The Good Fight, The Good Doctor, or The Good Place. As mentioned above, The Good Daughter is an adaptation of a bestselling novel that was published in 2017. The story is about two adult sisters who are reunited after a violent attack in their Georgia hometown brings up memories of the murder that tore them apart.

Slaughter herself adapted her book for the screen, penning the teleplays for all six episodes. Steph Green stepped in to direct and executive produce the series. As for the cast, here’s who you’ll see on The Good Daughter and who they play.

Sam, quintessential eldest daughter, left town and became a patent lawyer.

Rose Byrne’s breakout role in Hollywood was as a titular bridesmaid in Bridesmaids, though she had already appeared in films like Sunshine, Troy and Marie Antoinette at that time. That same year, 2011, she played Moira MacTaggert in X-Men: First Class and would reprise that role in a later film. She’s still best known for comedies, however, like Spy, Neighbors, and Neighbors 2 as well as horror movies like 28 Weeks Later and the Insidious films. Television-wise, Byrne played Gloria Steinem in Mrs. America and starred in both Physical and Platonic. She was nominated for an Academy Award for If I Had Legs I’d Kick You and a Tony Award for Fallen Angels.

Meanwhile, younger sister Charlie stayed in town and became a defense attorney.

Meghann Fahy was one of the breakout stars of The White Lotus’ second season, though she also hadn’t come out of nowhere. Prior to the HBO seires, Meghann made her Broadway debut in the Pulitzer Prize winning musical Next to Normal as an understudy and replacement for the character Natalie, then played Sutton on The Bold Type, and also acted in 83 episodes of One Life To Live. Since the HBO series, she has starred in the movie Drop and the Netflix series Sirens as well as The Perfect Couple and Your Monster. She is also a two time Emmy nominee, for both Sirens and The White Lotus.

Audrey Grace Marshall and Drew Cheek play Young Sam and Charlie

Flashbacks show who the sisters were before the trauma that shaped them.

Before The Good Daughter, Audrey Grace Marshall played Young Cassie on The Flight Attendent and was supposed to be in the Buffy the Vampire Slayer reboot. She did play Viv in the Fairly Oddparents live action reboot thing in 2022. Drew Cheek, on the other hand, has only one previous credit: a movie called F*ck Valentine’s Day.

Rusty is the sisters’ father and a former defense attorney with a reputation.

Brendan Gleeson is an Academy Award-nominated and Emmy Award-winning actor. He won the latter for playing Winston Churchill in the miniseries Into the Storm. You’ve seen him in the Harry Potter films, Braveheart, 28 Days Later, Gangs of New York, Troy, In Bruges, Edge of Tomorrow, Paddington 2, and The Banshees of Inisherin. Brendan has not done much television, but did lead Mr. Mercedes from 2017 to 2019, appear in the 2022 series State of the Union, and play Silvermane in Spider-Noir.

Lenore is Rusty’s secretary and the girls’ surrogate mother figure.

This is Harper Steele’s first ever professional acting credit. She is an Emmy winning comedy writer who worked at Saturday Night Live for 13 years and was a head writer for four of those years. Her other screenwriting credits include Eurovision Song Contest: The Story of Fire Saga and The Spoils of Babylon. Harper recently appeared with Will Ferrell in the road trip buddy documentary Will & Harper about their friendship before and after she came out as transgender.

Kelly is a suspect in a school shooting who Charlie chooses to represent.

Shayna Bernardo’s previous credits before The Good Daughter include the Lifetime movie Don’t Sweat The Small Stuff, an episode of Doom Patrol, and the horror movie Sorority Secrets.

Judith is an English teacher at a local middle school.

Paula Malcomson is probably best known for playing Katniss’ understandably checked-out mother Asterid Everdeen in The Hunger Games movies. She was also a series regular on the Battlestar Galactica prequel series Caprica, as well as Deadwood and Ray Donovan. She also recurred on Sons of Anarchy.

Huck is a teacher at the local middle school with a grudge against Rusty.

Jake McDorman is best known for leading roles in shows like Quintuplets, Greek, Manhattan Love Story, Limitless, Murphy Brown, The Right Stuff, Mrs. Davis and Dopesick. He also had a recurring role on Shameless. Movie-wise, you’ve seen him in Happiest Season, Lady Bird, American Sniper, Live Free Or Die Hard, Bring It On: All Or Nothing, and, of course, Aquamarine.

Olivia Williams plays Harriet ‘Gamma’ Quinn

First of all, no, this Harriet was the sisters’ mother and a genius physicist. Spoiler alert: she was violently murdered when Sam and Charlie were young girls.

You may know Olivia Williams from films like Rushmore, the 2003 Peter Pan, Anna Karenina, and An Education. Fun fact: Williams played Moira MacTaggert in X-Men: The Last Stand. She played Camilla Parker-Bowles on two seasons of The Crown and her other television credits include Dollhouse, Manhattan, and Dune: Prophecy. She’s also a prolific stage actress and starred as Nurse Ratched in One Flew Over The Cuckoo’s Nest at London’s Old Vic Theatre this year.

Ben is Charlie’s estranged husband, and also a lawyer. So many lawyers!

Michael Dorman is an Australian actor with many starring roles on television to his name, including shows like Wonderland, For All Mankind, Patriot, Territory, and Joe Pickett. His film credits include Leigh Whannell’s The Invisible Man and Pirates of the Caribbean: Dead Men Tell No Tales.

#6852. AI Platform Behind 12,000 E-mail Breaches Dismantled

The platform moved quickly. Since launching in February, EvilTokens had been tied to more than 12,000 compromised inboxes across over 10,000 organizations worldwide, according to Microsoft. Its victims included businesses and institutions in financial services, healthcare, construction, real estate, wholesale distribution, and higher education.

Microsoft and its partners seized 50 websites used to operate EvilTokens and disabled more than 150 additional domains supporting the service. The Metropolitan Police Service in the United Kingdom also arrested two men on Sept. 11 in connection with the alleged operation.

At the center of EvilTokens was an AI chatbot designed specifically for cybercrime. Once attackers gained access to an inbox, the chatbot could analyze messages, map relationships, identify employees involved in payments, and recommend ways to impersonate trusted contacts.

Microsoft said the platform helped criminals determine "who to target, who to impersonate," and how to get the most money from a compromised organization.

Business e-mail compromise has traditionally required attackers to manually comb through messages, learn how an organization handles payments, and find the right person to impersonate. However, EvilTokens simplified the entire process by automating much of that work.

The tools could quickly sort through a stolen inbox, summarize or translate messages, find invoices and wire-transfer discussions, and identify the people who moved money. EvilTokens could then draft messages that sounded like they came from the victim. It packaged all of this with mailbox access, phishing tools, management dashboards, and even customer support.

The service was sold through Telegram for a $1,500 startup fee, followed by a $500 monthly subscription. Microsoft's complaint also described a bulk e-mail tool that cost $600 a month and a more advanced e-mail delivery platform priced at $1,000 a month.

To break into a target, EvilTokens relied on device-code phishing. Victims were sent to a real Microsoft sign-in page and persuaded to enter an authentication code. The login process looked legitimate because it was, but the code quietly authorized a session controlled by the attacker.

That also made cleanup more complicated. The attackers stole OAuth tokens and authenticated sessions instead of passwords, so a password reset alone might not remove their access. Administrators also needed to revoke the stolen tokens and active sessions.

According to Microsoft's complaint,  EvilTokens used fake Microsoft 365 security notices and other branded templates to direct users into the device authentication flow. Once inside, attackers could use Microsoft Graph and other APIs to search and monitor a mailbox.

The company also found that AI played a role in creating EvilTokens itself. Investigators said much of the platform appeared to have been "vibe coded," with its developers using AI to quickly build and expand the service. EvilTokens reportedly drew on multiple AI models, including improperly integrated services from Groq and OpenAI.

The court authorized Microsoft to take control of or disable domains connected to the operation. Microsoft worked with Cloudflare, Coinbase, OpenAI, Railway, SpyCloud, the Shadowserver Foundation, and TRM Labs on the disruption. Health-ISAC joined the case because healthcare organizations were among the targets.

This marks the Microsoft Digital Crimes Unit's 40th court-authorized disruption and its first targeting an end-to-end, AI-enabled cybercrime service.

EvilTokens' infrastructure may be offline, but the model behind it is unlikely to disappear. The operation showed how AI can turn a stolen inbox into an immediately useful fraud playbook, allowing less-experienced criminals to launch attacks that once required considerable time and skill.

For more information, go to the Microsoft blog post.

Chris Paoli (@ChrisPaoli5) is the associate editor for Converge360.

#6848. 9 Bedroom Makeover Tips for More Sex

And we’re starting with things you already have.

We may earn commission from links on this page, but we only recommend products we love. Promise.

Your partner carries you into the bedroom à la your favorite #BookTok MMC, only for you both to remember the duvet is still covered with laundry that needs to be folded. Buzzkill. Or maybe you reach into your nightstand drawer for your wand vibrator, only to graze a stack of unpaid bills. Never mind…

“Your bedroom is already shaping how you feel about sex,” says Ashley Giddens, interior designer, artist and founder of Ashthetics NYC. It's not just clutter that can pull you out of the moment, or a tidy space that can help you stay in it, either, she says.

“Light impacts how you feel, which matters a lot when you're about to take your clothes off, while the textures impact what you feel beyond skin,” Giddens explains. Not to mention, the colors on the walls, sheets, and even the ceiling can influence how cozy, intimate, or downright sexy your bedroom feels, she says.

With that, a little bedroom makeover can help set the stage for more (and better) sex.

According to Philadelphia-based ASSECT-certified sex therapist and clinical licensed social worker Melanie Greenberg, LCSW, CST, MEd, Founder of Pulling Threads Therapy, changing your surroundings isn't a guaranteed libido booster. “But having a bedroom that is decorated and designed to make you comfortable, free of worry, relaxed, and connected to your senses and partner may make it easier to get in the mood,” she says.

Rest assured, you don't need a huge budget to bring it to life. Whether you've got $20 or $20,000 to spare, there's a way to make your bedroom a little sexier. Here are nine expert-approved places to start.

You might think a bedroom designed with sleep in mind and one designed for, well, doing it are two different beasts. But actually they are the same side of the coin: “If your bedroom doesn't help you sleep, you're probably not going to have much energy left for anything else,” Erica Corpening, co-founder and designer of Hidden Honey Homes, an adult-only boutique retreat emporium, tells Cosmopolitan.

On a physiological level, skimping on Zzz's can disrupt our endocrine system, potentially interfering with the hormones that support sexual functioning in the first place. That's why before you start shopping for silk sheets and sex swings, Corpening suggests that when designing a sex-friendly bedroom, “start with sleep.”

First and foremost, that means getting a comfortable mattress—whatever that means to you. In particular, however, take a beat to consider your preferred squishiness, how much the mattress squeaks when you move, and the weight limit. After all, if you can't get comfortable lying still, odds are you won't enjoy getting busy in missionary.

It also means considering the bed height you enjoy most. “A low platform frame can make the room feel grounded, relaxed, and a little more lounge-like,” says Corpening. It can also make getting into floor-based sex positions a whole lot easier. But it might not be accessible for people with hip or knee issues, or anyone who has difficulty getting up from low surfaces. On the flip side, a higher bed frame can give your room a more elevated, hotel-suite feel, while also making certain standing sex positions easier to pull off.

The lowest-cost suggestion on the list doubles as the most essential: Tidy up. “One of the sexiest things you can add to a bedroom is space,” says Corpening.

The debris of life (i.e., burp cloths, bills, yesterday's boxers) can keep your mind focused on everything on your to-do list—a serious turn-off (or sexual brake) for anyone whose desire is easily derailed by distraction. And without all those visual reminders of your responsibilities, your brain might finally have the bandwidth for something a little less responsible.

“So before you buy anything, clear off the nightstands and rescue the corner chair that's been holding last week's laundry,” says Corpening. And don't underestimate the wonders that under-the-bed storage, vacuum-sealed storage bags, and drawer organizers—all of which are just an Amazon order or Target run away—can do for your daily detritus (and, by extension, your sex life).

Materially speaking, what do you need on hand before, during, and after sex? Maybe you're a sensation seeker like yours truly and need a Wartenberg wheel, blindfold, or feather tickler to transition from your PG life to your X-rated one. Maybe you squirt, or are into golden showers, and like to lay down a water-resistant blanket before play. Or maybe you're a lube lover who would benefit from keeping a bottle within arm's reach rather than buried in the bathroom cabinet.

Whatever your pleasure preferences, take stock of your sex-specific supplies, then do what you can to make them as easy to access as possible.“Even small adjustments to a real setting can make a difference,” doctor of human sexuality Emily Nagoski, PhD, sex educator and author, writes in her 2024 book Come Together.

“It’s not super-sexy to have to get out of bed, dripping with fluids, and go into the bathroom to get a towel, especially on a cold New England night,” she writes. Her solution? Allocate a drawer, reachable from the bed, with towels dedicated to post-sex cleanup. “Some problems are just that easy to solve,” she writes.

Sex furniture, more or less, falls into two categories. First, there is the stuff that looks like, well, the kind of sex furniture that outfits a dungeon or BDSM playroom. I’m talking St. Andrew’s sex crosses, spanking benches, and sex horses. All of which, for the record, can be great bedroom makeover investments if they support your particular fantasies.

But there's also more discreet sex furniture, like sex pillows and wedges, which are primarily designed to support your bodies and make certain positions more comfortable. While unassuming, these seemingly simple additions can have a serious impact because “with better support, sex naturally improves,” Danny Griffin, the creative director of Liberator, tells Cosmopolitan.

“A firm, stable surface at the right angle takes the strain off knees, wrists, hips, and lower backs, so you can stay in a position long enough to actually enjoy it,” he says. Meanwhile, the few inches of lift provided by a pillow or wedge can significantly change how two bodies line up, he says, “which can mean deeper penetration or an easier path to the spot that matters, which is a big deal for couples with different heights or body types.”

Ultimately, whether you opt for a piece of furniture that screams sex dungeon or one that passes as a regular ‘ole cushion depends on your wants, needs, desires, and budget. To figure out what you should invest in, Corpening suggests, “Ask each other what you'd actually enjoy exploring, what sounds intriguing, playful, adventurous, or more comfortable. Then choose something that supports that experience.”

According to Giddens, another cheap change with a big payoff is a lighting revamp. “Bright light is inspection light, and it mostly puts you on display to yourself, which can make it hard to feel sensual,” she says. Bright lights have also been linked to feelings of unsafety, which is totally incompatible with good sex, she adds.

Your move: “Retire the overhead after dark, put two lamps low and close to the bed, and fill them with warm dimmable bulbs,” Giddens suggests. Specifically, look for bulbs rated 2700K or lower. “2200K gets close to candlelight,” she says.

And if you're going for more sex-party than spa-night vibes? “Swap one lamp to a pink or red bulb, or hang a small neon sign, and let that colored glow wash across one wall,” she suggests.

When choosing a color palette for your pleasure palace, take a cue from the bisexual Pride flag: “Pink is the sleeper pick,” Giddens tells Cosmopolitan. Associated with both calmness and intimacy, the hue can help create a more inviting atmosphere, she says. “It also sits in the same family as flushed cheeks and lips, so it tends to flatter skin,” she says.

“Red is the obvious answer, and it earns its reputation,” she says. On the walls, red can make a room feel smaller, “which in a bedroom reads as close and intimate,” she says.

For a moodier, more boudoir-like feel, consider deep jewel tones like plum and midnight navy, which make a room feel more enveloping, particularly when the lights are low, says Giddens. In fact, if you're tempted to go full black-walled for the ~drama~, she suggests using these colors instead. “They will give you the same moody depth with some warmth still in it, unless a little dungeon energy is exactly the goal,” she says.

Whatever shade you choose, don't commit based on how it looks at high noon. Instead, tape a few paint swatches beside your bed and see how they look under the lamps you actually use at night. “Warm bulbs can change paint a lot. Reds, pinks, and plums tend to get richer, while a cool blue or gray that looks crisp in daylight can go flat and muddy,” she says.

Before you rinse your brushes and fold the tarps, consider giving the ceiling a little love, too. “It's the move that almost nobody makes,” says Giddens. Yet “it's the surface you look at most when you're lying in bed, it's usually still builder white, and there's no furniture to work around, so a gallon of paint and a weekend afternoon gets you a completely different room.” Cheers to efficiency!

Of course, the color you use on the ceiling matters. “A ceiling bounces that color back down onto your skin,” she says. “My one hard rule is no green on the ceiling, because that same reflection casts green onto faces and bodies, which is about as unsexy as color gets.”

A saturated pink or a warm peach above the bed, on the other hand, can cast a warm, flattering glow on the naked bodies below. Red, meanwhile, is better left to the walls or smaller accents, as Giddens says it can feel heavy and intrusive overhead.

Good news: If the thought of spending your weekend in coveralls with a paint roller makes you want to crawl under the covers (to sleep), you can still join the color play. Same goes if your landlord has a strict No-Paint-Allowed policy. “If paint is off the table entirely, change your sheets,” suggests Giddens.

“Sheets are the biggest piece of color that sits right next to your skin, and fabric reflects color the same way a ceiling does,” she says. As such, she suggests picking them the way you'd choose a makeup shade. “Pink, peach, deep red, and plum bounce warmth back onto your face and body, while gray-green does the opposite.”

A well-placed sex mirror can turn an otherwise innocent situation downright indecent faster than a well-timed 'good girl' can make me forget my own name (just me?).

Sure, there's the tried-and-true mirror-above-the-bed approach. Though, word to the wise: Don't leave this one to any ol' Taskrabbit. A broken mirror might mean seven years of bad luck, but a broken mirror above your bed could mean a trip to the ER. So make sure yours is professionally and securely installed.

But you have other options, too. “If you place a mirror across from a chaise, or beside a doorway, play can spill into other parts of the space,” says Corpening. “You might catch a bare shoulder in your peripheral vision, or the two of you moving together in the glass, and then it's gone. It can heighten your awareness of your body and build anticipation without anyone saying a word,” she says.

Meanwhile, a mirror near a window can serve double duty. “If you hang it near a window, it bounces daylight around and makes the room feel open during the day,” says Corpening. A win for afternoon nookie fans, indeed.

#6847. Redesigned Microsoft Copilot Will Build Apps and Work on Its Own

Micorosft has announced that it's redesigning Copilot, the company's all-encompassing AI assistant, so users can hand off complex tasks, build tools by describing what they need, and assign work to an agent that keeps going after they log off.

The new Copilot experience has three main parts: Home, Code, and Autopilot. It also brings Word, Excel, and PowerPoint into the Copilot app. For users, the pitch is simple: Start a task in one place and stay there as it grows from a quick question into a document, an app, or a longer-running project. Most of the new capabilities are still rolling out or in preview.

Home puts Chat and Copilot Cowork together. Chat remains the place for quick answers, research, and drafts. Cowork handles work a user wants to delegate, such as preparing a briefing or pulling together a response to a request for proposals. Microsoft said Copilot eventually will route requests to Chat, Cowork, or Code on its own.

Office in Copilot will allow users to create a Word document, Excel workbook, or PowerPoint deck without leaving that experience. The files remain editable in the regular Office apps, and colleagues can work on them at the same time. Someone building a presentation, for example, could draw on a workbook, draft the slides, and keep refining both files from within Copilot.

Code is aimed at people who need a tool but might never open a development environment. A user can describe a tracker, dashboard, app, or workflow in plain language and have Copilot build it. Microsoft calls these "small, purpose-built solutions." The feature uses the same underlying technology as GitHub Copilot and runs in a sandboxed environment. A separate Copilot Managed Runtime, now in preview, lets organizations host and share the resulting apps within Microsoft 365.

Then there is Autopilot, previously known as Scout. It is designed for work that does not end with a single response: following a discussion, checking in with stakeholders, or keeping a project moving over several days. "Give it a name, a role, and a goal," Microsoft said. Autopilot runs in the cloud and has its own identity, memory, and workspace in the customer's tenant. Microsoft said its actions are subject to permissions, auditing, and organizational controls.

Those longer-running tasks also change how customers pay for Copilot. In a separate pricing post, Microsoft said everyday work in Chat and Microsoft 365 apps will remain covered by a per-user subscription. Cowork, Code, and Autopilot will be billed separately through usage-based Copilot Credits. A Copilot user subscription will still be needed to access those features.

For enterprise customers, the features that charge by usage will stay off until an admin sets up a spending policy in the Microsoft 365 admin center. Microsoft said there will be no charges before then. Admins can set budgets for the whole organization or for specific groups, limit how much individual users can spend, and get alerts when costs approach a set amount. Reports will show who is using the features and which tasks are driving costs.

Microsoft is bringing Code and Copilot Managed Runtime under those cost controls alongside Cowork. Copilot Studio agents are expected to follow in October. Before turning the features on for more employees, IT departments will need to decide who can use them, how much they can spend and which tasks are worth the extra cost.

Home and Code will begin rolling out through Microsoft's Frontier early access program in the coming weeks. Code's Frontier rollout starts at the end of September, when Autopilot also expands to private preview.

For more information, visit the Microsoft site.

Chris Paoli (@ChrisPaoli5) is the associate editor for Converge360.

#6840. You Can Definitely Do These Easy Nail Art Designs at Home

Take it from a fellow amateur manicurist.

We may earn commission from links on this page, but we only recommend products we love. Promise.

Ah, the at-home manicure. Nail artists make it look so easy when you’re in the salon, but get in front of a file and dotting tool at home? Suddenly, things can get a little shaky. I’ve technically been doing my own nails since I was about six, but really started leaning into trying to get better at painting on little designs over the past few years. I can say from experience: There is a lot of so-called easy nail art out there that, in reality, is very difficult to do yourself. But there are also plenty of designs you can totally pull off as a beginner. That, my friends, is what we’re here for today.

Unfortunately, the old “practice makes perfect” thing does actually apply to any type of creative endeavor, manicures included. I’m not saying you’re going to be able to perfectly recreate any of these looks the first time you try (though, if you can, maybe quit your day job). There’s a reason I still pay a professional to do my nails if I want them to look extra super good for a special occasion; those people have years of training I’ll never be able to catch up to. Instead, these are designs with elements I know a beginner can do well enough that you won’t get so frustrated with the results that you give up on your new hobby for good.

“It can be super intimidating to start painting your nails while you're looking at an inspo pic or video from IG or Pinterest, but remember that the reason you probably started painting your nails was to have a creative outlet and a moment of self expression,” nail artist and wise person Sigourney Nuñez says. “Try to not let perfectionism get in the way of something that is meant to be a self care moment where you get to show up for yourself in this fun, creative and colorful way.”

Hear, hear! If you’re ready to get started on your artistic ungual journey, keep scrolling for expert-recommended tips, tools, and plenty of design inspiration.

While your toolkit can grow exponentially the further you get into your nail art journey, Nuñez says the “perfect DIY tool trifecta” is a clean up brush, dotting tool, and liner brush.

I can confirm the clean up brush will be your most-used tool when you’re learning to do your own nails. Even if you don’t make a massive, colored-outside-the-lines mess, going around your nail with a clean up brush dipped in nail polish remover “can really give you that crispy salon finish at home,” Nuñez says. She uses a Simply Simmons #4 painter’s brush you can buy at Michael’s, but says you could also sacrifice an old angled eyeliner brush if you aren’t looking to spend money on tools yet. “Just be sure you don’t use it on your eyes again after you’ve dunked it in nail polish remover,” she warns.

Nuñez calls dotting tools “the gateway to the nail art universe.” You could use bobby pins, a flat eraser on the end of a pencil, or the end of a brush, but there are plenty of affordable dotting tool options too. Olive & June makes a nice double-sided one that would be a great first dotting tool, while Halo Taco has a fun, holographic set that comes with a total of eight different sized tips.

Once you’re really ready to level up, get yourself a nail brush. There are sets with multiple brush sizes out there, but Nuñez doesn’t think you need them. “I literally only use one type of liner brush, [the] Liner 3 from Bio Seaweed Gel,” she says. “It’s been my go-to brush for years.” Instead of dealing with a bunch of different size brushes, you can make different size lines by varying the amount of pressure you use.

I cannot emphasize this enough: If you’re serious about wanting your manicure to look as close to professionally done as possible, you absolutely must prep your nails before you even think about picking up your base coat. This means completely removing all your polish, cutting and/or shaping your nails with a file, and cleaning up your cuticle area... but not too much on that last part.

“When it comes to DIY prep, less is more,” Nuñez says. “There is no need to over trim any surrounding skin unless there's a hangnail that's waving hi at you.” Instead, she recommends using a cuticle buffer stick like the Sally Hansen Push & Buff 2-in-1 Cuticle Pen. “It helps exfoliate the cuticle area and side walls, plus helps buff out the waxy layer that can accumulate [on your nails],” Nuñez says. “That waxy layer is totally normal but it gets in the way of adhesion.” I like to wash my hands after I’ve done all that trimming, filing, and buffing, to make sure there’s no leftover nail dust that could ruin my hard work.

Keeping your cuticles healthy between manis will make this prep step easier. “I’m actually so passionate about cuticle care,” Brooklyn-based nail artist Anna Miles says. “A little attention every day will do wonders—more than any nail tech could accomplish in our monthly sessions.” She recommends using a towel to gentle scrub your cuticle area after a shower (”to encourage the cuticle skin to roll back”) and be diligent about using hand cream and cuticle oil after you wash your hands.

“If you’re like me and out of sight equals out of mind, buy travel sizes in multiples and keep one everywhere—bag, coffee table, bathroom, bedside,” Miles says. She loves the CND Rescue Rxx Daily Keratin Treatment and Zoya Healing Dry Skin Hand & Body Cream, both of which come in smaller sizes.

The number one thing that will make your nail art look better is to learn to leverage your fulcrum finger (aka the digit that’s holding your hand steady while you work; Nuñez says this is usually the pinky) and elbows. Do not try to do your nails while sitting on a couch; you need a table with enough space to create those stabilizing points.

Whether you’re going to use a dotting tool, liner brush, sponge, or other nail art tool, it can take some practice to figure out how much polish to put on it before you start designing. I always do a test run on a piece of paper; it’s not a perfect substitution for painting on your curved nail, but I think it gives you a pretty good idea of how much polish to use. Nuñez also has a handy video on how to load your liner brush properly.

And, again, you just need to practice. When I’m in the habit of doing my nails every week, I’m shocked at how much better I get over the course of a month. Ready to dive in? Here’s some inspo to get you started.

Let’s start with the easiest nail art of them all, a skittles manicure. This is simply painting each nail with a different shade of polish, which is a fun way to start playing around with color theory—a key skill for professional-looking DIY manis. “Every great manicure starts with a beautiful color story,” Nuñez says. “When the colors can do most of the talking, you don't have to try as hard with your DIY design.”

Glitter polish is kind of a nail art hack in its own right, because the texture makes any mistakes kinda blend in. For this look, I swiped some random splotches of purple cream over a glitter from Mooncat (a brand that makes some of the best sparkles, IMO).

The half and half manicure is a great way to practice creating straight lines: Since you fill in the space on one side of the line, it’s less obvious if things are little crooked.

“When painting lines, instead of moving the brush, you can try rotating your hand/nail while keeping the brush anchored,” Nuñez advises. “This can help with a more precise application.”

I know, I know, all these straight lines look intimidating, but, similar to the half and half, I promise that the fact that you fill in the triangles after you paint on the lines makes it far easier to get a crisp look. And if you have the patience, you could always wait for the base color to try, then use Scotch tape to stencil out the details.

Marble looks complicated, but Miles swears it’s not so difficult. “Marble is good for beginners because the success of this design is in the imperfection,” she is. “Uneven, shaky lines and blurry, smeared areas... sounds like the makings of a gorgeous marble!” Pick two colors of polish with high contrast to make the marble design stand out.

Miles says there are many ways to do marble, but one of the first methods she fell in love with is using an unused kitchen sponge that’s been cut into thin slices. Start by applying one of your polishes as a base coat, then paint the other color onto a nail-sized section of your sponge slice. “You can see the marble ‘veins’ on the sponge as you paint it,” Miles says. Stamp the wet paint onto your nail, using a rocking motion so it covers the whole nail. You can add metallic accents or a shimmery top coat to really make it special.

This is one of my go-to designs. You paint on two coats on your base color, then go in with a second color but stop before you get too close to your cuticle. It’s extremely simple, but gets loads of compliments.

If you crave more intricate designs but don’t have the brush skillset just yet, stickers are a great option. “I love using nail art stickers,” Miles says, noting that her favorites come from Deco Beauty. “For best results, make sure to apply them to a long-dried base color and paint two thin top coats instead of one thick one if you’re using traditional polish.”

Polka dot nails stay trendy, so that dotting tool is definitely worth the investment. There are many different ways to do dots—a bunch of super tiny dots, big circles, a mix and match of multiple size. I’d recommend practicing on a piece of paper first so you can get the pressure just right for the size you’re looking for. A light touch is key once you’re actually on the nail. “If your base color is not fully dry and you put too much force on the dotting tool that can create a dent and hole in your hard work,” Nuñez says.

This is a design that definitely requires Nuñez’s recommended stabilization techniques, but if things get a little shaky, you can just say you were going for a Bambi effect. Larger dots can also be a bit more forgiving.

Another trick for adding details when you don’t have a super steady hand is to apply the color you want for the detail as the base coat and then layer thick strokes of another color on top. That’s what I did for this look: The blue polish went down first, then I just painted one strip of green vertically and one strip horizontally to create the rectangle accent effect.

As everything gets digitized, I fear we’ll lose important ancient techniques, like using paper hole protectors as a stencil for a negative space half moon effect. At least, that’s how I’d pull off this look.

In the vein of nail stickers, confetti polish is a great way to create nail art without even looking at an extra tool. (KB Shimmer makes really fun ones that come in a bunch of shapes and colors.) Just make sure to apply two thin coats of top coat so the sparkles stay put.

I would never in a million years suggest that a French manicure is easy. But I will tell you that those rounded silicone stamper tools absolutely work to create a micro French, especially on shorter nails.

Using a rhinestone application tool can be a bit tricky to master, but I do find it a relatively easy way to add just a touch of detail to your nails. These won’t stick with just some good top coat; you’ll need to use nail glue too.

Great news: Once you’ve gotten good at polka dots, you can totally do flowers too. These are made up of just five black dots and one white one, but the final look seems much more complicated.

#6839. AI Leaders Warn of Risks at United Nations Security Council Briefing

The heads of OpenAI, Anthropic, and Hugging Face recently joined AI researcher Yoshua Bengio at the United Nations headquarters in New York on September 23 to warn the UN Security Council about the security risks of increasingly capable AI.

They did not offer a single plan for managing those risks. Bengio called for licensing and independent safety reviews. OpenAI's Sam Altman proposed common standards that countries could adopt through their own laws. Anthropic's Dario Amodei urged AI developers to slow down when safety work cannot keep pace. Hugging Face's Clément Delangue warned that limiting access to capable systems could leave defenders at a disadvantage.

France, which held the Security Council's rotating presidency, convened the meeting on AI and international security. The discussion exposed a difficult question for governments: how to oversee technology that crosses borders when countries disagree about who should set the rules.

Bengio, co-chair of the UN's Independent International Scientific Panel on AI, challenged the idea that competition forces companies to keep building more powerful systems. "The race is not a law of nature: it is the product of choices," he said in his published remarks. "They've told us they would slow down if they could. They can."

He called on developers to show independent experts that frontier systems are safe to train and deploy. He also proposed incident reporting, licensing, and liability insurance. Bengio identified catastrophic misuse, concentrated power, and the loss of human control as risks that require international cooperation.

Altman acknowledged the danger of moving too quickly. "We have unilaterally slowed down in the past. We will do so in the future," he said, according to remarks published by OpenAI. Decisions about AI's direction "cannot be made by labs in San Francisco alone," he added.

Altman proposed complementary national and international standards to measure frontier AI capabilities, assess risks, and test safeguards. He said countries should be able to compare evidence and verify commitments. He also called for faster reporting of serious incidents and secure channels for sharing emerging threats. Such standards should accommodate both open and closed models without favoring established companies, he said.

Amodei made a more direct commitment about the pace of Anthropic's releases: "We will slow down as much as necessary," he told the council. He identified two principal concerns: the misuse of AI to develop biological weapons and the possibility that advanced systems could outstrip developers' ability to control them.

The Anthropic chief proposed placing outside evaluators inside AI companies with access comparable to employees. He suggested that governments begin with a narrow agreement against using AI to make biological weapons, then develop ways to evaluate models and verify commitments across borders. He also called for common testing standards and a system for notifying other countries of significant AI incidents.

Delangue drew on a July incident in which Hugging Face disclosed an AI agent cyberattack. He called for stronger monitoring and disclosure, but argued that safety also depends on giving defenders access to capable tools. The central danger, he said, was "asymmetry of powerful AI" between attackers and defenders, large companies and everyone else, and wealthy countries and the rest of the world. His team used an open-source model while responding to the attack, he said.

The U.S. government opposed a broader international regulatory role. White House science adviser Michael Kratsios told the council that countries should develop their own ability to use and govern the technology. In the U.S. statement, he rejected new global governance structures and used the phrase "superintelligence, formerly known as AI," adopting President Donald Trump's terminology from his General Assembly speech the previous day.

The four AI briefers differed on how far oversight should go and how widely capable systems should be available. They agreed on a narrower point: Governments need better ways to understand what advanced AI systems can do and to detect when safeguards fail.
