#6712. The “Brainy Heel” Is This Season’s Shoe MVP

Every product on this page was chosen by a Harper's BAZAAR editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

They’ll make every outfit feel more thoughtful

Sometimes, you just want to dress for yourself. It’s not about man-repelling so much as just responding to your own needs: I want to feel good, look well-dressed, and be drawn to what I’m wearing—for me, not them.

Looking at the new fall/winter 2026 shoes, from the feathered hiking styles at Prada to the peaked-toe lace-up shoes at Jil Sander to the grown-up Mary Janes at Chanel, I found myself coming back to that thought. These are heels for me, not them: attractive, but not designed purely to attract; chic, but not flashy; and, dare I say it, made with the wearer’s comfort in mind.

They’re shoes that make you think—and make you wonder what the woman wearing them is thinking too. You’re intrigued by her sartorial choices, especially her heels. Brainy heels? Perhaps. But like the woman herself, maybe they don’t need a title. Just a formula.

As with any well-designed object, what makes these shoes appealing is the law of proportions—the relationship between shapes that creates a visual balance. And what makes them compelling is that there’s always something just a little off, something to make you look again or make you wonder why, something that opposes and attracts at the same time. We’ve written about the wrong-shoe theory, the comfort-first commuter shoe, and plenty of the season’s chicest styles. The brainy heel brings them all together in one slightly off-kilter package.

Below, I’ve pulled together 15 pairs of shoes that fit the bill.

#6710. 13 Clever Details I Saw at the 2026 Kips Bay Decorator Show House in Dallas

Check out the first ever home gym in Kips Bay Show House history.

The seventh annual Kips Bay Decorator Show House in Dallas returns to 2999 Turtle Creek Boulevard, but don’t expect it to look anything like it did in 2025. A new group of the country’s leading designers has taken on the 25,000-square-foot French Renaissance estate, transforming each room to showcase bold ideas, highlight exceptional craftsmanship, and reveal the upcoming trends we can expect to see in the world of interior design.

Visit the show house and see each of the 22 designers’ rooms, from the first dedicated home gym in Kips Bay history by Dallas-based Platinum Gym Design, to a small but mighty European-inspired kitchen by Stephenson Home, to a 1970s-inspired primary bathroom fit with a glass brick shower and curtained-off water closet by Nuance Interiors. With sponsors like Benjamin Moore, The Shade Store, The Rug Company, and more, the vibrant and personality-filled spaces come as no surprise.

As always, proceeds from the show house go to the Kips Bay Boys & Girls Club, which provides educational, enrichment, and developmental programs to thousands of young people, as well as Dallas-based nonprofits Dwell with Dignity and The Crystal Charity Ball.

To see this celebration of exceptional design, you can purchase tickets here and visit the house from Saturday, November 14th to Sunday, December 6th. However, if you can’t make it to the Lone Star State in time, check out some of our favorite moments from the event below.

Kelsi Lamberth of Nuance Interiors pulled inspiration from the 1970s for her primary bathroom, separating the shower from the main prep space using a curved glass brick wall. The sculptural aspects can be found throughout in this groovy space.

In the primary bedroom, Troy Rivington of Rivington Marx Interiors cocooned the room in a dark floral wallpaper and accessorized with other moody botanicals throughout. He was inspired by the romance of 1940s Cairo, dressing the room up like it belonged to a well-traveled collector.

As the first ever dedicated home gym at the Kips Bay Show House, Peter Hoffman of Platinum Gym Design chose a refined, luxurious setup. He named the room Tanren after the Japanese art of hammering and refining metal, and used walnut in both the equipment and the ceiling slats, stainless steel frames, and brown leather accents to complete the exercise space.

In this bespoke bathroom, Russ Peters and Bill Cates of Peters Cates Design lined the wall behind the Studio McGee x Kohler bathtub with a rich merlot tile by RIAD Tile. It’s a beautiful statement and a fabulous way to bring more texture and dimension to the room.

In this tucked away library space, filled with collected finds, designers Christina Garcia Lysaught and Grayson Knight of Layered Dimensions finished off this dark space with a stunning statement mirror. Around the perimeter are resin busts of wildlife, like owls and deer, bringing in a bejeweled touch to the room.

The small but mighty kitchen, designed by Katie Stephenson of Stephenson Home, is crowned with a crackled-gold blooming wallpaper, bringing a warm balance to the rest of the room. It’s covered in birds, butterflies, and blossoms, and it wraps the cook space in a golden glow, reminding all who enter that this room should be as beautiful as it is functional.

The citron paint color wrapping the walls in Jen Bienvenu’s entrance hall is undoubtedly enchanting and serves as the perfect backdrop for her “After the Hunt” theme, but it’s the lampshades made from old silk saris that tie everything together for us. They provide such a magical warmth and really capture the essence of a collector’s treasure room.

Related Story: Almost Every Piece in This Layered Arkansas Home Was a Lucky Antique Find

Step through the citron entry and enter the round room, designed in collaboration by Helen Bergin Interiors and Lichten Architects. The walls are softened by long curtains from The Shade Store, which create beautiful juxtaposition with the curved, sculptural silver media console by Asher Gillman. The actual turntable is flush to the surface, giving it a seamless look while it emits wonderful sound.

In the Withdrawing Room by Jen Stevens of Fonde Interiors, plaster botanicals by artist Amanda Norman come to life as they crawl from the mantel along the chimney breast, escaping to the ceiling. The vines were handmade by Norman and capture the essence of a garden left undisturbed, showing that this formal-at-first-glance room has a wilder side.

The rosy palette in designer Jen Dean’s—founder of Jede Interiors—grand bathroom is topped off with a hand-painted floral mural that covers the ceiling. The rose-gold shine reflects the natural light in the room and give it more depth, while the natural motifs spread across the rest of the space.

Inside this observatory bedroom by sisters Amy Mobley and Katie Norrid of City Hill Design, the Verellen bed is raised up from the ground on a custom platform, emphasizing the importance of the furniture’s role in this space meant for relaxation and rest. It’s wrapped in a striped silk carpet that beckons your touch with a chartreuse mohair sofa at the foot.

Related Story: A Trip to the Lone Star State Inspired the Earthy Textures of This Tennessee New Build

Colorful and collected, this vibrant library by designer Betsy Wentz is not visually quiet. The maximalist space is covered in pattern-mixed Schumacher from ceiling to floor, and the designer pumped up the volume by opting for this triangular table in this tucked away corner.

In designer Amy Switzer’s textural bar lounge—filled with plush chairs, unique artwork, large tassel accessories, and beaded cigarettes—it’s the stone details that really make the room sing. Onyx cornices crown the windows, blue agate inlays decorate the floor, and a banquette which curves around a custom stone and wood table bring a natural coolness to the room that only stone can bring.

#6706. This Playful Florida Raised Ranch Is Proof That Bold Style Can Be Incredibly Timeless

Classic materials and a modern layout read as "traditional with a twist."

How do you turn a straightforward ranch-style home into something exciting and unexpected yet timeless? When a young family of four asked designer James Farmer to do just that for their central Florida abode, he opted for a gut renovation, using his homegrown knowledge of Southern style, years of interior expertise, and pointed enthusiasm to renovate ranches to create a highly functional and striking escape for his clients.

Located in an old, established neighborhood on a lake in Winter Park, Florida, the home was surrounded by mature live oaks and beautiful landscaping. “I would describe the area as old-school Florida with a touch of Mayberry,” says Farmer, referencing the fictional town from The Andy Griffith Show. The home itself was humble and honest—“no hiding its hand at the poker table,” says Farmer.

Alongside Skolfield Homes, Farmer kept the architectural style generally the same, with the most impactful changes being a complete overhaul of the galley kitchen and a relocation of the staircase from the side of the house to the middle, putting it in its deserved place as a focal point of the living room. The new kitchen is what he calls a “glorified galley,” with a sunny breakfast room off to the side that used to be a screened-in porch. He then reinforced the home as a "front door house," ensuring the formal entry space would actually be used rather than passed through by adding a bar perfect for fancy cocktail parties or a casual post-work martini.

For the actual interior, the designer layered various textures and bold patterns, combining them with hallmarks of grand Floridian style, like floral wallpapers, gold details, intricate trim, palm motifs, pecky cypress wallcoverings, and a coastal color palette. The line between indoor and outdoor living is further blurred with trellis details and a selection of timeworn brick and wood flooring. "We really leaned into this in a new way with this project because of its location," he says. "True, classic Florida can be so fun but also timeless, and that was our goal with this project."

Farmer encouraged his clients to splurge only on materials and pieces that'll truly stand the test of time, opting for classic finishes and prints that will age with the young family—an approach he details intimately in his newest book, Home Again: A Return to Gracious Interiors. Ultimately, the decadent interior paired with the modern structural changes naturally created what ended up being Farmer's motto for the home: "Traditional with a twist!"

FAST FACTSDesigner: James Farmer of James Farmer DesignsLocation: Winter Park, FloridaThe Space: A ranch-style home with 4 bedrooms and 3.5 bathrooms

Fabulous cocktail parties start in this formal space before migrating to other parts of the house. With no TV in the space, all eyes are drawn to the bar set behind a custom set of accent chairs by Thibaut, which is where Farmer's clients shake up (not stir) "the best martinis in Florida."

A color palette of peach, robin's egg blue, and butterscotch is celebrated in this room, the designer explains. By painting the ceiling in a blue tone—a trick commonly seen on Southern porches—he gave the room a subtle indoor-outdoor touch commonly seen on Southern porches.

Borrowed light from the kitchen and stairwell shines on the rich velvets and great patterns in this family room. “The color combo of terracotta and emerald with creamy white millwork creates a handsome and elegant palette,” Farmer adds.

Once a screened-in porch, Farmer kept the alfresco feel by covering the walls and ceiling with trelliage panels and outfitting the room with timeless white wicker furniture by Memoire Design (once known as Mainly Baskets Home). Vintage elephant garden stools now stand as whimsical side tables.

“We kept the original corner cabinets and wainscoting but gave it a bit of glam with a custom Gracie Studio wallpaper and lacquered ceiling,” Farmer says of the dining room, which kept its original footprint. The console tables are painted in a lemony yellow hue to match the window treatments and the room's other cheerful elements.

Farmer broke up this completely new, double galley kitchen into sections to make hosting a breeze. On one side of the counter is the seating and hangout zone, while the other side plays host to a functional working kitchen, outfitted with a Wolf range and Sub-Zero fridge. Think of it like traditional with a twist; "These clients are fabulous entertainers, and the island can also make a great buffet for parties," he says. "We like to think that The Golden Girls would thrive in this kitchen… Mid-century meets Maw-Maw!"

Old Chicago brick and palm motifs in the wallpaper make this breakfast hub feel more like a terrace than an interior enclave. Farmer created this room as part of the kitchen expansion—its bay window only enhances the porch-like vibe he wanted. "Sometimes geometry is your best friend," Farmer says, noting that the rug, table, and even shape of the room are octagonal. "You might not notice it, but it makes a moment."

“We were able to move the staircase in this house, which allowed for a more efficient floor plan for this young family,” he explains. “When you are under a major renovation, you can think outside of the box, or sometimes inside the box, just as easily.” Now the stairwell can live as a beautiful moment all its own at the center of the home, rather than pushed to the side.

The daughter was sleeping in a crib when Farmer first started working on his clients' house, so he wanted to design a room that could grow with her (by the time the project wrapped, she was in her big girl bed). The pink trellis wallpaper by Schumacher perfectly rides the line between feminine and elevated.

Touches of bamboo, palm, and pecky cypress introduce that old Florida feel, reminiscent of family vacations to the family's favorite spots in the Sunshine State, without being too on the nose.

“An interior design trick that is so old-school, but still so fun, is using a drapery fabric for wall covering and vice versa,” Farmer explains. “We were able to bring that from the primary bedroom to the primary bathroom.”

James Farmer is a Southern author, interior designer, and speaker known for his ability to create beautifully familiar and welcoming homes. To James, a home engages all of the senses—the pleasantries of colors and materials, the feel of the doorknob warmed by the sun, the smell of fresh laundry, the sound of the closing door, and the taste of supper on the table. Through his design firm, James works with clients to truly create a home.

Projects by James Farmer Designs can be found from the countryside of Connecticut to James's home state of Georgia. Though his work can be found throughout the country, James proudly has built his business in his hometown of Perry, Georgia.

#6705. Catherine Zeta-Jones’s Daughter Carys Borrows Her Mom’s Lacy Gucci Top From the ’90s

A gem from the beloved movie star’s closet

When your mom is Catherine Zeta-Jones, there is hardly a ’90s or Y2K gem you can’t get your hands on. From turn-of-the-century Fendi baguettes to red-carpet gowns (that crimson Versace dress from the 2004 Oscars particularly comes to mind), the Academy Award-winning actor embraced all the best of the decades’ trends. And now, her daughter gets to as well.

Yesterday, Carys Douglas, the 23-year-old first-born child of Zeta-Jones and husband Michael Douglas, went through her mother’s archives for a night-out look perfectly suitable for a Gucci x Moda Operandi cocktail event in New York City. Her choice was a sheer long-sleeve lace top from the Italian fashion house, with an elongated, deep U neckline secured solely by a single clasp. The piece, with its raw hems, was a familiar pull—last seen on the Chicago star at the 1996 premiere of The Long Kiss Goodnight in Los Angeles.

At the ’90s event, Zeta-Jones layered the top over a little black cami and paired it with slim trousers in the same sheer lace. She added a large cross necklace, chunky rings, and a pair of open-toe heels, and slung a large bag over her shoulder.

Three decades later, her daughter put her own spin on the piece, wearing it with a little black miniskirt and semi-sheer black tights. Douglas took a cue from her movie-star mom’s glam, wearing her hair down in slept-in waves and focusing her makeup on the lips, adding a pop of color. She grabbed a quilted Gucci bag to finish things off.

This was not the first time Douglas borrowed something from her mom’s closet. In 2024, she celebrated her 21st birthday in a pink Emanuel Ungaro slip previously seen on Zeta-Jones at the 1999 MTV Movie Awards. After posting the look on Instagram, her famous mom lovingly commented: “You deserve it sweetheart.”

#6704. Rihanna Pairs Her Golden Jacquard Coat With a Pair of Baggy Blue Jeans

Another day, another Rihanna sighting at Giorgio Baldi. She finished things off with a pair of thin, rectangular sunglasses for a last low-key touch.

With this endorsement from Rihanna, and recent stamps of approval from other stars like Jennifer Lawrence, it appears Dries Van Noten coats are going to rule the outerwear game this season.

#6703. From “Magnetic Merlot” to “Soft Licorice,” 10 Dark, Minimalist Manicure Ideas to Try This Winter

Every product on this page was chosen by a Harper's BAZAAR editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Bolder hues are getting the starring role as winter sets in

While soap manicures, subtle petal nails, and longevity techniques once dominated the summer months, a darker color palette is quickly taking over now that the clocks have gone back and evenings are early. Yet if you're hesitant to venture into "too bold" territory or fear a pop of color on your fingertips, a dark manicure doesn't have to feel overly attention-grabbing.

In fact, as professional nail technician Samantha Rudge shares, dark manicures with a more minimalist finish are proving to be winter's most popular manicure trend. "For this season, I think we'll be seeing darker classic manicures with minimalist touches," she shares, adding that she has already seen an uptick in shade requests for Bio Sculpture's rich, chocolate-toned Decadent Cocoa and the ever-popular Garnet Noir, a moody red-black hue. "Nail designs like chrome, cat eye, micro French tips, and tortoiseshell will also dominate this season," she adds.

"Short nails are also very popular right now, no matter the nail art or shades," she adds. Regardless of shape or color, Rudge stresses the importance of prioritizing nail health at this time of year. To protect nail longevity and prevent chipping, Rudge recommends assessing your natural nail condition, choosing the right base (BIAB or gel), and using a nourishing cuticle oil to repair and strengthen brittle nails.

Ahead, discover 10 new dark manicures for those who love a more minimalist finish, from “Garnet Noir” nails to subtle lines.

Somewhere between a square and an oval, these squoval nails look sophisticated and smart—plus, paired with the deep red “garnet noir” shade, they make a strong case for opting for a shorter length.

The epitome of minimalist nail art, add subtle intrigue to your manicure this winter via a curved line created on the cuticles. Choose complementary shades to recreate it: think cherry red with hot pink; navy blue and khaki green.

Taking over the SS27 runways this season, purple is finally having a moment in the spotlight. When it comes to nail colors, look for richer hues with a jewel-tone finish to keep the manicure elevated and modern. Rudge recommends Bio Sculpture's Erica shade.

All shades of wine are a staple as the festive season approaches, but why not add a chrome effect for extra sparkle?

Coffee tones are becoming one of the most popular color stories in nail salons this winter. These teeny-tiny dots make for a unique switch-up and can be created using a micro dotting tool.

A subtle marble effect elevates an otherwise classic burgundy manicure.

While almond and square are among the most popular of nail shapes, don't underestimate a soft oval. Paired with a classic cabernet tone, this dark manicure strikes the balance of looking impactful yet soft.

If you're taking a break from acrylics or longer lengths, a super-short licorice manicure is the way to do it. Add a glossy top coat and keep the nails short and square.

In the mood to experiment? Simply switch up each nail with chocolate swirls, block colors, or a French tip to recreate this look.

This ultra-glossy midnight blue manicure is all about a high-shine, mirrorlike finish that could be created with a chrome powder or top coat.

#6701. Before Anything Else, Geoffrey Holder Was a Painter. A New Show Spotlights His Tremendous Body of Work

Geoffrey Holder refused to choose a lane. Born in Trinidad and Tobago in 1930, he was a principal dancer with the Metropolitan Opera Ballet, the first Black man to win Tony Awards for both directing and costume design (for the Broadway hit The Wiz), and the voice and face of 7Up throughout the 1970s and ’80s, all while building a celebrated film career with roles in Doctor Dolittle, Annie, and the Bond film Live and Let Die.

And yet, before everything else, Holder was a painter. “Painting always came first,” says his son, Leo Holder, who oversees the artist’s estate. “Practically everything else he did has always been an extension of this. He was always working on something. At any given time, wherever he was living, there were several canvases in progress.” Still, across his decades-long career, it was the facet of his work that received the least attention.

“Geoffrey Holder: Black Magic,” on view at the Museum of Contemporary Art North Miami from November 18, 2026, to April 4, 2027, aims to correct this. Guest curated by Dr. Erica Moiah James, principal investigator and publication editor for the Geoffrey Holder Project, it’s the first museum survey to center Holder’s work as a visual artist. Bringing together more than 60 works across eight decades, the show offers an unprecedented look at the practice that anchored his entire multidisciplinary career.

Geoffrey Holder, Untitled, n.d. Oil, oil marker, and China marker on wood. 84 x 36 inches.

Influenced by his older brother, Boscoe Holder—himself a painter, designer, dancer, and musician—Geoffrey took up painting in childhood and had his first formal exhibition in Trinidad by the age of 14. According to his son, Holder funded his move to the United States in the early 1950s by selling 20 of his paintings, and when he died in 2014, he left behind more than 250 paintings and 300 collages, along with a trove of drawings, sculptures, and photographs.

The exhibition is the result of more than a decade of work, beginning when James was first invited by Holder’s family to see his archive in 2015. “I knew Geoffrey the way most people knew him,” James tells Vogue. “I had seen his films. I knew he was involved with The Wiz, I had seen the 7Up commercials. But I didn’t know him. What I realized the moment I stepped into his archive is that he knew himself. He understood there would be a moment when people would get it. He might not be there, but he was going to select and preserve the record of his life so that we could really dive into it.”

Geoffrey Holder, Waiting for the Dance, 2001. Oil, pastel crayon, and China marker on Masonite. 48 x 48 inches unframed. 50 ½ x 50 ½ inches framed.

The show takes its title from Holder’s deep interest in what he dubbed “magical people,” those whose creative spirit and sheer presence carried an unmistakable energy. “There’s a negative way of framing Black people as ‘magical Negroes,’ but he wasn’t thinking that way,” James explains. “He thought of it in incredibly positive terms, and was referencing a je ne sais quoi—something almost beyond language, a sort of confidence and way of being in the world that is truly remarkable. So we took the word ‘magic’ and racialized it, because most of the subjects in the exhibition are Black people.”

Occupying the full museum, the show is organized into five sections—Portraits, Epistles, The Sacred and the Profane, Genre, and Figure Studies—pairing the artworks with a rich collection of costume sketches, performance footage, correspondence, and memorabilia that provide historical context while illustrating the breadth and depth of Holder’s creative life.

One standout is Portrait of Carmen (1980), an oil on canvas depicting a stylized Black woman in a sleek black dress and turquoise earrings against a crimson background. The painting reflects not only Holder’s interest in the female figure but also his profound devotion to his wife, the American dancer, choreographer, and actress Carmen de Lavallade, to whom he was married for 59 years, until his death. Also on view is the portrait Woman in Yellow (1985), part of a motif that, according to James, traces back to the first time Holder saw de Lavallade, wearing a yellow dress. “He painted women in various stages of a yellow dress forever,” she says.

Geoffrey Holder and Carmen de Lavallade in 1955, the year they married.

Also central to the show is a series of works that reinterpret biblical stories through Holder’s distinctive lens. Adam and Eve (ca. 1990s), for instance, is a towering diptych in oil, oil marker, and china marker on canvas, in which black tree trunks rise through a dense forest of green leaves; in the lower left corner, two nude figures are nearly swallowed by the foliage. In another large oil painting, Adam and Eve (2000–2005), Holder returns to the same subject, this time creating a more intimate image that foregrounds the couple while still enveloping them in greenery. Holder’s son explains that the artist’s interest in biblical narratives was not, strictly speaking, religious. Instead, “he saw them as a wonderful source of storytelling, theater, and drama.”

Geoffrey Holder, Adam and Eve, 2000-2005. Oil on board. 71 7/8 x 30 inches unframed. 73 ½ x 31 ¼ inches framed.

In another biblically inspired work, John the Baptist and Jesus (undated), Holder depicts the two figures as Black men standing together in cerulean water, sharing a tender moment as a crowd looks on. “This is one of my favorites, because it shows a level of intimacy between Black men—a level of care, gentleness, and tenderness that you just don’t [usually] see out in the world,” James says. “Geoffrey is making a point. He’s appropriating the religious story, but there’s something very contemporary, maybe even a longing for that kind of open expression of care among Black men in the world.”

Reflecting on the interest in his father’s visual art practice more than a decade after his death, Leo Holder says he’s more convinced than ever that, as a multihyphenate, his father was ahead of his time, rarely seen as a serious painter because of his many other creative pursuits. “Nowadays, we have far more of an ecosystem that allows you to be polymathic. And he’s gotten the kind of attention for his art that he never got when he was alive,” he says. “In part, it’s because now everybody’s caught up.”

#6700. The 10 Best Hair Wavers Bazaar Editors Are Actually Using

Every product on this page was chosen by a Harper's BAZAAR editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Get beach hair all year round with these top-rated tools

As someone with pin-straight Asian hair, I have always admired waves and curls. I have slick, straight, stubborn hair, and—most frustratingly—it resists holding a curl. I’ve tried every trick in the book: extra-hold mousses, setting my curls with pins, and the best strong-hold hairsprays. Nothing seemed to do the trick—until I discovered hair wavers, specialized curling irons that create bends instead of twists.

In recent years, hair wavers have grown in popularity because they're easy to use and deliver effortless-looking results. “The effect [of a waver] is less curled and more textured. It’s great for that effortless, tousled vibe or adding body to finger hair without that much bounce,” says celebrity hairstylist Danielle Priano. Ahead, Priano shares her picks for the best hair wavers, along with some of the Bazaar beauty team’s favorites.

Every time my hairstylist uses this waver at the salon, I notice that my waves can stay for up to three to four days, which is considered a long time for my hair type. The waves also look so natural that they give me that “just permed my hair” effect. Priano is a fan of the tool too, saying, “It heats evenly and creates long-lasting waves with a sleek finish.” Plus, it has tons of features like a two-hour auto shutoff and a clear digital display.

You may have seen this on social media, and for good reason: At under $50, it is a great option for budget-conscious users. It’s also highly rated for both long and short hair and is easy (and safe!) for beginners to use because it has no exposed barrel. We also love that it comes in 1- and 1.25-inch wave sizes, so you can choose the wand that best fits your length and style preference.

Priano also swears by Trademark Beauty’s double-barrel wave iron. “This one’s loved for its wider barrels and even heat distribution,” she says. “It’s great for longer hair or when you want that soft glam wave.” As a bonus, the tool also works well for shorter hair as it creates two waves rather than the usual three.

This is my go-to hair waver at the moment because it’s so versatile. You can adjust the barrel size to get the exact wave you want, from tight to soft and beachy. (And you also have full control over the temperature.) I’m super lazy, so I like to opt for something that has full capabilities in one.

You can’t beat this price, which makes Bed Head’s tool a great entry-level styler. That, along with its tourmaline ceramic barrels, 10 heat settings, and easy digital controls, makes it super straightforward to use. The barrels themselves are also on the large side, which creates a great beachy effect in the final look. And if you’re accident-prone, take comfort in the fact that it comes with a protective glove, too.

“This waver is pricey, at nearly $200, but it’s also the most versatile. It comes with three barrels—a waver, a rod, and a clamp—so you can create any type of wave or curl your heart desires,” says Bazaar beauty director Jenna Rosenstein. “I have thick hair, and it works just as well on my hair as it does on those with fine, thin hair, since the heat is customizable up to 450 degrees. It’s also great to travel with!”

This one is another Bazaar editor favorite: “While I do own two different hair wavers, they both serve a unique vibe. I love this INH Triple Barrel when I’m going for a looser, more lived-in wave,” says Sarah Olivieri, Bazaar senior designer. “It really gives the illusion of salty beach hair freshly dried from the sun.”

“I love the look of effortless waves, but creating seamless waves without creases or harsh demarcations between crimps can be tricky,” says Bazaar beauty market and commerce editor Katie Intner. “I love the jumbo Mane waver because the large barrel size gives a more natural wave and crimps hair in under a few minutes. Plus, it’s dual voltage, which is great for travel, and gives a surprising amount of wave longevity.” Even better, the base is detachable and can be used with any of the brand’s other hair tool attachments.

This waver is on the pricey side, but that’s because it has the brand’s next-gen features. Think: even heating and a curved—not cylindrical—shape on the barrels that gives that ideal “effortless” look. They’re also finished with an anodized coating that adds shine and reduces frizz. Who doesn’t want that?

If you aren’t a bona fide hairstyling expert and want a hair waver that doesn’t require much technique, this option from WavyTalk might be a good fit. It has a built-in timer you can set from four to 10 seconds that beeps when it’s time to move on to the next section of hair. Its deep V-shaped plates and dual-barrel design sculpt hair for a more pronounced wave.

Luckily, it’s “fairly straightforward,” Priano says. “You start by sectioning your hair; the smaller the section, the more defined the wave. Then, working from the roots down, clamp the waver onto the hair and hold for a few seconds, usually 5 to 10, depending on your hair type and heat setting. Move down the same strand, overlapping slightly to continue the wave pattern all the way to the ends. Repeat throughout the entire head, and once cooled, you can brush it out for a softer, more lived-in texture or leave it defined for more drama.”

What’s the Best Way to Prep Your Hair Before Using a Waver?

To minimize potential damage, you’ll want to do a few things first: “Always start with clean, dry hair; wavers are not meant for wet styling. I recommend applying a heat protectant from mid-lengths to ends to shield the hair from damage,” Priano adds. “If you’re looking to boost volume and hold, add a lightweight texturizing spray or a mousse before blow-drying. And don’t forget to detangle. Smooth, tangle-free strands help the waver glide better and create more uniform results.”

Is There a Difference Between a Waver and a Curling Iron?

“The main difference between a hair waver and a curling iron lies in the shape and result. A curling iron wraps hair around a single barrel, creating more classic, rounded curls or ringlets, depending on the size of the barrel and the technique,” Priano says. “A waver, on the other hand, presses the hair between barrels to form deep, wave-like ridges.”

#6699. The 12 Best Round Brushes for a Salon-Level Blowout

Every product on this page was chosen by a Harper's BAZAAR editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Perfect your next hair look with these expert-loved picks

Perfecting an at-home blowout once required contortionist-level abilities combined with a little patience and a lot of skill, especially when styling the back of your head. But nowadays, thanks to the advancement in hair tools—from high-powered blow-dryers to one of the best round brushes—it's easier than ever to craft sleek styles that rival your local salon without dealing with arm fatigue.

Unlike regular boar-bristle or vented brushes, round brushes feature a cylindrical shape in which hair wraps around for a seamless dry. Though many of these tools are crafted from similar materials, they're also available in a range of sizes to suit your hair texture and unique beauty needs. But regardless of the brush you choose, experts say a polished dry isn't achieved with dripping wet hair. "The key is to get all the moisture out," celebrity hairstylist Priscilla Valles tells Bazaar. "To achieve the smooth look, you need to keep drying until the hair is completely dry."

New York City-based hairstylist Jackson Simmonds recommends having two to three round brushes in your collection—just in case you change your hairstyle. “To keep it simple, think, smaller brushes for shorter lengths, bigger brushes for longer lengths,” he explains.

Here, we've narrowed down some of the best round brushes from Oribe, Sisley Paris, and more, all highly recommended by experts. Whether your hair is short, extra long, curly, or super fine, these top picks can be game-changing to help deliver a bouncy, full-bodied blowout from the comfort of your bathroom.

When we polled hairstylists, several mentioned Olivia Garden's hairbrushes. They win best overall round brush because they're foolproof: They come in a full range of sizes (general rule of thumb: the longer your hair, the larger and longer your barrel should be), and it's hard to get your hair tangled in an Olivia Garden round brush. If you need a place to start, the Ceramic + Ion Thermal Hairbrush is a bestseller. But you can also go through the brand's hairbrush guide to find your perfect fit.

Bristle type: syntheticBarrel material: ceramicSize options: 0.5 inches to 4.25 inches

A boar-bristle round brush is ideal for someone with coarse hair texture, as it provides more stretch and tension than a ceramic or ionic brush. This line is a favorite of stylists like Simmonds, who says, “They have a huge selection of sizes, bristle density, and length, so you have an option for whatever style you're trying to create.” He adds that the cork handle is a plus: “It makes it light and breezy in your hand and never slips away as you're working.”

Bristle type: nylonBarrel material: woodSize options: 35 to 85 millimeters

Oribe crafted a brush that's as efficient as it is pretty. Atop the stunning resin handle is a mix of boar bristles and nylon pins that move through hair without damaging or tugging and help distribute the natural oils in hair for more shine. And although it is an investment (the handle is hand-finished), this is a tool you’ll use for years to come.

Bristle type: boar, nylonBarrel material: Italian resinSize options: medium, large

If you're in search of a round brush with frizz-control benefits, look no further than this top-rated, ergonomic favorite from Drybar. After all, who better to go to than the blowout blueprint? Its bristles are optimized with ionic technology to mitigate frizz while providing salon-level tension control. It even has a thumbprint grip to help maximize your styling experience.

If you're already a fan of Moroccanoil's coveted hair staples, allow us to introduce you to its top-rated ceramic and ion-infused round brush. It heats up when in use with a blow-dryer, making drying and detangling hair a breeze. Designed for medium to long hair, the large barrel covers a good amount of surface area without tugging. The bristles are heat-resistant vegan boar bristles, and they're pretty densely packed, so you can get a grip and lift on the hair while still allowing strands to glide through without pulling. The bristles also aren’t overly stiff, so they won’t scratch your scalp as you’re styling. And we’re always here for a no-slip grip!

The soft bristles provide hold for easy drying and also keep the hair super soft. We also appreciate that it has scalp-stimulating bristles, so it feels especially nice when you use it at the root.

Plus, it features an ergonomic handle for extra comfort, which you’ll love whether you’re new to blowing out your hair at home or just need the extra grip. Once you get the hang of things, you may decide to upgrade, but it’s a great starter brush.

The three sturdy knobs on the handle of this round brush mean you don't have to worry about the tool accidentally slipping out of your hands mid-blow-dry. Plus, the placement of the bristles in Philip B.'s tool makes it easy to give your roots an extra lift. With a mix of boar bristles and nylon for extra reinforcement, it’s a sturdy brush that’s earned stellar reviews for good reason.

Can a round brush be chic? This one could be mistaken for a found object with its teak handle. Simmonds recommends the Japanese-made brand, saying that it offers “quality brushes that deliver results, look great coming out of my kit, and last for ages with proper care.” The mix of boar bristle and heat-resistant nylon pins is also ideal for optimal hold and shine.

This brush in particular helps seal the cuticle for an extra-smooth and shiny blowout, and can even work for extra-long hair. Have short hair? Try the 25mm version for the same luxury experience.

Bristle type: syntheticBarrel material: ceramic coatingSize options: 25mm–53mm

Like anything worth learning, styling your hair with one of the best round brushes takes practice. "Ask your stylist if they’ll do a lesson," offers Gamez. It also might be helpful to reference a YouTube or TikTok tutorial. The general guidance is: start fresh out of the shower, detangle your hair, and then begin blow-drying and brushing small sections of hair, working your way around your head. You can start in the front or back sections. Professional hair colorist Tylor Johnson recommends starting with the front sections around your hairline, as your hairline pieces are the most important and require the most precision, and you don't want to be fatigued when styling them.

What Are the Benefits of Using a Round Brush and Blow-Dryer?

It’s all about the shape. “The round brush allows the hair to wrap around it, creating a bend in the hair as it dries and cools,” explains Mohapi, who explains that a regular hairbrush should not be used for heat-styling. “Expensive brushes like Masons are not designed for direct heat, and you will ruin a perfectly beautiful brush,” says the stylist.

Several top stylists prefer to use round brushes and blow-dryers separately rather than an all-in-one device, as it enhances control while minimizing hair damage. "Blow-dry brushes tend to be high heat, so if you’re putting that tool on soaking wet hair, you’re going to overdry and damage it," explains Gamez. "A blow-dryer allows you to create space between the heat and the hair shaft as needed. You can control the air direction of a dryer with your nozzle, pointing downward and sealing the cuticle, resulting in shinier, smoother hair, as opposed to a hot brush that blows air outward in all directions."

On the other hand, blow-dry round brushes definitely have their benefits. According to Valles, one key factor is their overall ease of use to craft salon-worthy styles at home. "A blow-dry brush makes the process so much easier," Valles says. "Some people aren't as coordinated handling a brush and dryer simultaneously, but the blow-dry brush gives the same results and is easier to manage." At the end of the day, the best round brush for you is one you actually enjoy using regularly.

“Many people wouldn’t want to use a boar bristle brush for ethical reasons, and there are lots of good alternatives, so boar bristle isn’t the only option,” explains Mohapi. “It is, however, a highly effective material and is used extensively in hairbrush manufacturing globally.” Here’s why: “Boar bristles are highly tolerant of heat and won’t melt or buckle under the intense extremes of a packed Friday at the salon. The fibers are both strong and flexible and have the unique quality of being able to almost grab the hair and hold it in place as you pass it through the brush.”

That said, the stylist adds that some synthetics are nearly as good. They just may not last as long. “Nylon is a different thing altogether,” he notes. “Nylon will close down the cuticle and help create a shiny, slick finish to the hair. This is great for straight hair, but nylon brushes take all the body out of the hair, so when you use a nylon round brush, it has a different type of finish.”

#6698. 13 Fab Old Photos of 2026 Rock & Roll Hall of Fame Inductee Sade Adu

Think Sade, and a few things likely pop to mind. There’s the signature beauty look: the dark eyeliner, shapely brows, red lip, and long, slicked-back plait. There are the clothes: good earrings—often big gold hoops—and stage outfits that were sexy, yes, but also slyly demure; high-waisted trousers, semi-sheer turtlenecks, cropped jackets, qipao-style dresses. You may nerdily make the distinction between Sade the woman—Helen Folasade Adu, OBE, born on January 16, 1959, in Ibadan, Nigeria—and Sade the Grammy-winning band, formed in 1982 and made up of Adu, Paul Denman, Andrew Hale, and Stuart Matthewman.

Now there’s one more thing to add to that list: the Rock & Roll Hall of Fame. This weekend, the band will celebrate their induction—along with acts including Phil Collins, Billy Idol, Oasis, and the late Luther Vandross—in a ceremony at Los Angeles’s Peacock Theater. Now, will any of Sade’s members actually be present for that ceremony? No, according to a statement released on November 10. Even so, they called the recognition “both a gift and a timely reminder to give thanks, not only to those who voted for us but also every single person who lets our music into their lives.”

And what music that is: from “Smooth Operator” to “Kiss of Life,” “Is It a Crime” to “No Ordinary Love,” “The Sweetest Taboo” to “I Couldn’t Love You More,” Sade’s soulful, sui generis sound remains a benchmark for timeless cool.

Here, in celebration of her group’s latest honor, we’ve rounded up 13 striking old photographs of the divine Sade Adu, a generational talent and forever style icon.

Sporting spotty shirting, high-waisted trousers, and a wide belt during a 1984 set in Rotterdam.

This is no ordinary love–and those are no ordinary neutrals. A graduate of London’s Saint Martin’s School of Art, Sade shows off her style bonafides at the Hammersmith Odeon in 1984.

Cooler than cool in sunglasses and a simple white button-down.

Performing at the Live Aid charity concert at Wembley Stadium in 1985.

Gold jewelry, jaunty bolero jacket, can’t lose.

In textured whites—and that classic red lip—at the Ambassador East Hotel in Chicago, 1985.

Making her Saturday Night Live debut on December 14, 1985, with Tom Hanks as host. The band performed “Is It a Crime” and “The Sweetest Taboo,” both from their second studio album, Promise.

In 2010, recalling the release of Sade’s debut album, Diamond Life, in 1984, The New Yorker’s Sasha Frere-Jones poetically described Adu’s voice on as “a grainy contralto full of air that betrays a slight ache but no agony, and values even imperfect dignity over a show of pain.”

With the English singer-songwriter Leo Sayer at a charity ball in London, 2001.

Having a moment at the United Center in Chicago, 2001.

#6697. Mary Lovelace O’Neal Broke the Rules of Black Art

Marianne Boesky Gallery in New York mounts the late abstract artist’s first posthumous show, “Requiem”

Mary Lovelace O’Neal moved to New York in the late 1960s at the height of the Black Arts movement. The Mississippi-born artist enrolled at Columbia’s School of the Arts in 1968, where she honed her distinct take on abstract-expressionist painting. Blazing a trail as one of the few Black women in this field, she faced criticism from her peers, who felt her work should engage more directly with the political issues of the time, that it was not “Black enough.” She responded with her so-called “lampblack” paintings: radical pitch-black abstractions made with carbon soot, allowing her to retain her expressionistic sensibility while answering the social question of Black art.

But this response to socially conscious art through minimalism never fully defined her career. Lovelace O’Neal relocated to San Francisco after graduating from Columbia, where she reignited her exploration of color and abstraction. Her black color fields “unzipped,” as she called it, to reveal new, kaleidoscopic abstractions. By the 1980s and through the ’90s, Lovelace O’Neal developed her idiosyncratic expressionistic style, often meditating on tangible subjects through obscure, gestural paintings. Requiem for a Daffodil (1997), a six-by-11-foot painting, embodies this bold, high-energy approach. It features floral forms, from yellow bouquets to a field of delicate white flowers running across the top border, gathered together for what can only be understood as a funeral. Lovelace O’Neal’s vigorous brushstrokes streak across the canvas, yet the work retains a sensitivity, a delicate and caring touch for the subjects that populated her abstract works. It is perhaps the best representation of the middle part of her six-decade career. These two decades are the subject of the appropriately titled “Requiem” at New York’s Marianne Boesky Gallery, her first posthumous show on view through December 19.

“The painting Requiem for a Daffodil, to me, is an expression of the depth to which Mary could feel for her subjects,” gallerist Marianne Boesky tells Bazaar. “A funeral dedicated to a flower, attended by flowers, delivered with such solemnity, is a reflection of Mary’s acceptance and understanding of the loss of something beautiful, innocent, fleeting.”

Lovelace O’Neal died on May 10 at her home in Mérida, Mexico, at 84. Born in Mississippi and raised in Arkansas, she left home in 1960 to study under David Driskell at Howard University in Washington, D.C., before briefly attending Maine’s Skowhegan School of Painting and Sculpture in 1963. At this school, she devoted herself to abstract expressionism, studying the work of Franz Kline and Willem de Kooning.

Lovelace O’Neal lived what can only be called an exciting life, documented through archival material in the back room of Boesky Gallery. While living in California, she also traveled the world, collecting memories and experiences on jaunts from South America to Africa that informed her paintings. Panthers with Socks (1985–90) epitomized this process; the frenetic abstraction of a leaping white cat draws on both her 20s in New York and her 1984 trip to Morocco.

“In Panthers with Socks, the brushy figure of this leaping animal makes for a clear allusion to the Black Panther Party and the artist’s involvement in the Civil Rights Movement,” Boesky explains, “while the sights, sounds, and memories of the artist’s time in Morocco inspire the bold colors and theatrical architecture of the composition.”

Although Lovelace O’Neal leaned away from overt political work during the 1960s, her abstract painting never abandoned concerns about race and notions of Blackness. One example in the show, Juke Joint (1997), referenced the Jim Crow–era establishments where Black communities gathered, places Mary witnessed firsthand growing up in the southern United States. The gestural work conveys the movement of dancing legs, evoking the free spirit of these establishments.

The ’80s and ’90s were largely defined by her life as a teacher. The artist joined the Department of Art Practice at the University of California, Berkeley, as a professor in the ’70s and received tenure in 1985. “I wanted to pay tribute to her as both an epic painter and as a lifelong teacher,” Boesky says. To do so, the gallerist intentionally paired Lovelace O’Neal’s show with Michaela Yearwood-Dan, a 32-year-old Black abstract painter, who opened her solo show, “A Woman of Will,” the same night.

“[When I first saw Mary’s paintings,] it was really the first time I felt like I had really come across a female Black abstract painter who felt uncontained,” Yearwood-Dan tells Bazaar. “There was a real sense of freedom and lack of control, but in an intentional, free-spirited way.”

By juxtaposing these two shows, Boesky facilitates a cross-generational dialogue between the two artists, who, despite admiring each other’s work, never met in person. “Mary admired the energy and color in Michaela’s abstract paintings and quickly grasped Michaela’s commitment to activism, which also underpinned her paintings,” Boesky says. “Michaela had known about and followed Mary’s work, unbeknownst to me, at the time I began working with both of them. Completely different generations and geographies, but kindred spirits nonetheless. It felt like kismet to present their shows side by side.”

That the work still resonates across generations underscores Lovelace O’Neal’s lasting power. The paintings on view at Boesky reveal how the artist used abstraction to layer unmitigated expression and feeling into her subjects, from people and animals to place and memory. “She understood early that ideas and emotions had the potential to be conveyed more powerfully through abstraction than through identifiable narration,” Boesky says. Viewers can catch glimpses of a pig in What’s time to a pig (c. 1990s) or potted plants in A Tree Branch (c. 1990-91) amid the frenzied mark-making. Boesky says these images “stampede across a canvas or hover like a dream.”

Lovelace O’Neal offers a sneak peek into her dreams—her mind—through these paintings. The beauty of “Requiem” is that it compiles works from the undeniable turning point of her career. The works still feature the lampblack foundation while also capturing how color furiously reemerged in her practice. Seen for the first time since her passing, the work remains as potent as ever, a product of a life devoted to her idiosyncratic vision.

“She referred to the act of painting as labor, her life’s labor,” Boesky says. “And she referred to the paintings as her children, the product of that labor, put out into the world to make their own mischief.”

#6696. Hugo Boss CEO Daniel Grieder Steps Down Amid Frasers Takeover

Hugo Boss has announced that CEO Daniel Grieder will depart the German company, effective December 31. David Epstein — currently the chief brand partnerships officer at Frasers Group — will replace him, effective January 1, 2027.

Grieder was appointed CEO in June 2021, overseeing the company’s Claim 5 turnaround — a strategy focused on improving brand relevance, product innovation, digital output, omnichannel experience, and operational logistics to drive growth. Before Hugo Boss, Grieder spent more than 20 years at Tommy Hilfiger.

His mandate at Hugo Boss was to double company sales to €4 billion by 2025, believing the company had lost its relevance within the fashion space. He decided that while the brand’s heritage remained strong, it needed reframing. In 2022, he divided the parent company into two individual brands: Hugo and Boss — positioning Hugo for Gen Z and Boss for millennials.

Grieder also ushered in an ongoing collaboration with David Beckham, introduced in 2025 as a co-designed capsule. For fiscal 2025, Hugo Boss reported €4.3 billion in group sales.

“Looking back, what makes me proudest is what our teams accomplished over the past years,” said Grieder in a statement. “Together, we have brought Boss and Hugo back on stage, built a 24/7 lifestyle product offering, redesigned our stores worldwide, and renewed our culture and the way we work. I am deeply grateful to everyone who made this possible.”

Grieder continued: “With this strong foundation in place, I am convinced that now is the right time for me to step down and hand over to the next generation of leadership. It has been an honor to lead Hugo Boss, and I will always feel connected to its people and brands.”

Daniel Grieder and Boss ambassador David Beckham.

In May 2026, Hugo Boss sales fell 6% and have declined further since, attributing this to the latest phase in the Claim 5 turnaround strategy, “Claim 5 Touchdown”, which began in December 2025, focusing on brand equity, relevance, and long-term profitability over short-term volume growth. During Q3 earnings, the company shared new guidance for fiscal 2026, forecasting a high-single-digit decline, attributed to weakened demand and ongoing uncertainty.

In June this year, Frasers Group put forward a €2 billion (€38 per share) takeover proposal to Hugo Boss. The German company considered it “financially inadequate”, urging shareholders to reject it. However, Frasers continued to push, progressively upping its stake in the company. In August, Frasers increased its stake to 47.89%, edging closer to majority stakeholder status. Frasers Group CEO Michael Murray assumed the Hugo Boss chair position in September, after Stephan Sturm stepped down.

In October, the British retail conglomerate raised its stake to approximately 50.08% — an additional purchase of 198,666 shares, equating to €7.63 million, according to the stock market filing. The German fashion company became a subsidiary of the Mike Ashley-owned Frasers business. This year, Frasers has continued its aggressive M&A play, buying out Harvey Nichols, acquiring a stake in Under Armour, and upping its stake in Burberry. The Frasers portfolio also includes Flannels, The Webster, Sports Direct, and Slazenger, among others.

“On behalf of the supervisory board, I would like to sincerely thank Daniel for his dedication and his contribution to Hugo Boss,” said Murray in a statement. “Under his leadership, the company sharpened its positioning and renewed the way the brands connect with customers around the world. We respect his decision to step down.”

#6695. Rooney Mara Brings Boudoir Dressing to the Red Carpet While Arm-in-Arm With Husband Joaquin Phoenix

She wore an all-black “naked” look at a Gentle Monster launch event in NYC

Every product on this page was chosen by a Harper's BAZAAR editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Like Moira Rose or Wednesday Addams, Rooney Mara tends to stick to a strict black-and-white color scheme, especially when she hits a red carpet. And while it might sound monotonous in theory, her looks always accomplish the difficult feat of keeping things fresh.

That was certainly the case on Tuesday night, when Mara attended the launch event for Gentle Monster’s new Intelligent Eyewear collection, where she supported her husband, Joaquin Phoenix, who stars in a new Jonathan Glazer-directed short film tied to the smart eyewear line.

Mara stole the show for the evening, as she managed to take a trend we’ve seen ad nauseam—naked dressing—and imbue it with a bit of excitement.

Instead of the lacy slips or the chainmail netting we’ve come to expect from “naked” ensembles, Mara’s felt more playfully undone. She looked a bit like she had just stepped out from behind the boudoir, in the middle of disrobing, and these were the base layers left after everything else was stripped off.

Up top, the actress wore a lacy black bra layered underneath a corset-style cami with the straps left dangling off her shoulders for a less-polished effect. Then, for bottoms, she wore a sheer black maxi skirt from Khaite, which she layered over a pair of matching boyshorts.

The skirt was pulled from the label’s Spring/Summer 2027 ready-to-wear collection, and it featured a nude, ruffled band that decorated the bottom hem and continued up the back.

To match her shadowy ensemble, Mara slipped into a pair of inky patent-leather mules from Giuseppe Zanotti, while carrying a leather Antigona vanity bag from one of her go-to labels: Givenchy. Finally, she popped on a pair of oval-shaped Ivy sunglasses from where else but Gentle Monster.

Phoenix, in contrast, wore a very understated and buttoned-up ensemble consisting of a black sweater layered over a white dress shirt, and paired with jet-black slacks and Doc Martens. He also wore his own pair of round eyeglasses, courtesy of the host brand.

Sometimes, opposite dressers really do attract.

#6694. MoMA and Chanel Rolled Out the Red Carpet to Honor Steven Spielberg

It was a night full of unscripted surprises and picture-perfect moments at the Museum of Modern Art’s 18th annual Film Benefit on Thursday—fitting for an evening dedicated to the power of cinema. As stars and patrons began to take their seats, Stephen Colbert jokingly pulled out Jeff Goldblum’s chair and Martin Short embraced John Krasinski in a corner: vignettes that signaled the lighthearted and lively program to come.

As the lights dimmed, the crowd marveled as a montage showed the momentous filmography of this year’s honoree, Steven Spielberg: Jaws, Indiana Jones, West Side Story, Jurassic Park, Lincoln, and Minority Report, to name a few. Once the room was illuminated again, there was a collective gasp as the spotlight shone on Tom Cruise, one of the few names who wasn’t initially on the tip sheet for the occasion.

The actor recalled the formative and inspiring experience of watching Jaws for the first time, and cracked jokes about the less-than-desirable jobs he took solely to afford tickets to see Spielberg’s movies at the cinema. Cruise thanked the legendary director for “always being generous to [me] in films, but also being generous to [me] at home” as he, quite literally, jumped off the stage and beelined to his friend and longtime collaborator in the audience to share a long hug.

The sentimental speeches continued, with tributes from Martin Scorsese and Emily Blunt, as well as remarks from Christophe Cherix, MoMA’s David Rockefeller Director, and Rajendra Roy, MoMA’s Celeste Bartos Chief Curator of Film. “There are so many wonderful things about Steven,” Blunt said. “He listens in this beautiful way that you wonder if he’s not quite listening, but he’s capturing you.” And of course, Steve Martin and Martin Short also couldn’t let the night pass without indulging in a comedy bit, with the duo taking to the stage to deliver a hilarious introduction. By the time Ariana DeBose serenaded the room with “Somewhere” from West Side Story, the emotional roller coaster was almost complete.

As Spielberg accepted his honors, he praised MoMA’s film program and Chanel for their enduring support of the medium. He also made sure to thank Chanel for his suit, taking a moment to show off the details and the jacket’s impeccable lining. Spielberg concluded by thanking his wife, Kate Capshaw, for teaching him that “there’s more to living than what we do for a living.”

The guest list brought together Hollywood icons and newcomers alike. Drew Barrymore, who got her big break in E.T.almost 45 years ago, took her seat for dinner near comedian Marcello Hernandez, who was delighted to take a selfie with Michelle Pfeiffer. There was no shortage of those who’ve graced the screen in Spielberg’s box-office hits, either, from Ed Burns and Christopher Walken to Zoe Saldaña, Cate Blanchett, Eve Hewson, and Michelle Williams.

And just when one thought the night was winding down, Colbert stepped on stage to announce that Bruce Springsteen would be performing. After a sentimental introduction summarizing his friendship with Spielberg, The Boss launched into “Dancing in the Dark” right as dessert was served.

Stephen Colbert, Martin Short, Steve Martin

Emilie Goldblum, Steven Spielberg, Jeff Goldblum

Jane Rosenthal, Christy Turlington and Edward Burns

#6693. Only Law Roach Could Source a Look From Marc Jacobs’s Louis Vuitton x Richard Prince Collab

Law Roach’s latest archival pull for Kim Kardashian was for the fashion (and art) history buffs.

In October of 2007, Marc Jacobs sent models down the Louis Vuitton runway dressed as an army of over-the-top 1950s-inspired nurses. As seen on the likes of Naomi Campbell, Stephanie Seymour, Eva Herzigova, and Nadja Auermann, baggy plastic coats were layered over colorful dresses and cinched with leather belts, and black lace masks were embroidered with the house’s logo. Each uniform was styled with white tights and white heels, and topped with traditional nurse’s caps—stitched with red sequin letters that together spelled out “Louis Vuitton.”

The campy Spring/Summer 2008 collection, presented during Paris Fashion Week under the helm of then-artistic director Jacobs, was inspired by Richard Prince’s 2003 “Nurse Paintings” series. The pop artist, who rose to prominence in the 1980s, shared Jacobs’s love for a reference—and for this particular body of work, he transformed the covers of mid-20th century medical pulp novels into eery, gory paintings, with messy paint runs and blurry red splotches that read as blood.

The result was a vibrant commentary on society’s portrayal of women as both pure caregivers and sexualized fetishes—an idea which further came to life on the LV models.

(One such painting was even featured on the cover of a Sonic Youth album, 2004’s Sonic Nurse.)

Nearly two decades later, the seminal collection returned to the fashion discourse thanks to Roach (and Kardashian). The stylist, a dedicated archivist who has pulled rare vintage gems for everyone from Zendaya to Ariana Grande, dressed the All’s Fair actor as a Louis Vuitton nurse.

The Spring/Summer 2008 ensemble was complete with the season’s Papillon Frame bag, made in collaboration with Prince, and the duo even worked with milliner Stephen Jones on a custom “K” headpiece.

In September 2026, the actress married Cameron Fuller, an actor himself and someone very familiar with the entertainment industry as the son of a famous producer.

Here’s everything to know about Dynevor’s relationship with Fuller so far.

Fuller is the son of producer Brad Fuller, who specializes in horror films like The Purge franchise, A Quiet Place, and A Nightmare on Elm Street. The younger Fuller studied performing in college, receiving a bachelor of arts in theater from the University of Southern California in 2017. He has since appeared on shows like Insecure, Into the Dark, The Goldbergs, and The Last Ship.

In March 2023, Dynevor and Fuller were seen leaving a hotel in New York City together. A month later, they were photographed holding hands in London, eventually meeting up with Fuller’s father for some shopping. A few pics showed some PDA where Fuller’s arms were wrapped around the actress.

In July, they were photographed enjoying Wimbledon.

E! broke the news of their engagement on May 7, 2024, confirming that Dynevor unveiled her engagement ring at the 2024 Met Gala.

In 2022, Fuller was romantically linked to Pretty Little Liars’ Lucy Hale briefly. He also dated influencer Lauren Elizabeth for a few years. In 2021, Dynevor dated Saturday Night Live alum Pete Davidson for five months.

Yes. Dynevor and Fuller left the Corinthia Hotel together and headed to the BAFTAs on Feb. 18, 2024.

The Bridgerton star was nominated for EE Rising Star and was dressed in a white gown with a low-cut halter neckline by Louis Vuitton and a pair of gold sandals. Fuller wore a classic black tux.

Dynevor and Fuller were spotted at the US Open on Aug. 26, 2024, making their first public appearance since revealing their engagement. The pair looked smitten and stylish as they watched tennis matches together. The Bridgerton star wore a navy pinstripe vest with jeans, accessorizing with a small white purse. Meanwhile, her partner opted for a laid-back look in a white T-shirt, gray cardigan, and blue pants.

On April 8, 2025, Dynevor and Fuller posed together at the Los Angeles red carpet premiere of Drop. Dynevor stood out in a bridal white gown, while her partner was sharp in a black suit. Her long-sleeve gown featured a surprise leg slit.

In mid-December, Dynevor and Fuller made another rare public appearance together in Los Angeles, attending the after-party for The Housemaid following its premiere. The couple stepped out at TAO, where they were photographed with Sydney Sweeney.

Dynevor wore a black top paired with a matching skirt, while Fuller also opted for an all-black look.

Yes. Dynevor confirmed her marriage to Fuller on Sept. 17, 2026, nearly two weeks after the couple reportedly wed in Provence, France, on Sept. 5, 2026. She shared two black-and-white wedding photos on Instagram: one showed the newlyweds walking together after the ceremony, while another captured them kissing beneath a floral arch.

“Married my best friend🌙,” Dynevor captioned the post.

Earlier in the month, Fuller’s close friend, actor Sam Lerner, also shared photos from the wedding weekend on Instagram. The festivities included several of the couple’s famous friends, including Logan Lerman, Cole Sprouse, and Kiernan Shipka.

On Nov. 11, 2026, Dynevor was photographed wearing her slim gold wedding band with her engagement ring while running errands in London’s Notting Hill neighborhood.

In April 2026, Dynevor shared how she first met Fuller during an appearance on The Drew Barrymore Show.

“I did a film called Fair Play,” she said, “and I went to Sundance, and it was a big high in my career, it was like one of the first movies I’d done, and it was getting all this hype so I was feeling good, and there he was.”

She added, “It was at TAO, the pop-up. We were at the club.”

#6689. Make the Skirt Your Fall Personality

Every product on this page was chosen by a Harper's BAZAAR editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Thanks to Prada, the skirt has never felt fresher

The collection seen around the world this year was, by all accounts, Prada’s Spring/Summer 2027 show, which served as something of an edict on the skirt. Composed of 63 looks, each featuring a distinct iteration in varying colors and fabrications, the presentation was nothing short of a skirt master class from Miuccia Prada. Following that spectacle, you would have been hard-pressed not to spot a skirt on nearly every runway, be it the gold-leaf pencil skirts at Saint Laurent or the many offerings from Julian Klausner’s Dries Van Noten, which brought to light another unsung hero as of late: the ocelot print!

Though the silhouette felt decidedly new under the glamour of the spring collections, the truth of the matter is that skirts made a similar splash during the fall shows. However, the garment itself wasn’t necessarily the prevailing message. Rather, fall/winter 2026 signaled a return to a more considered approach to dressing, with perhaps no better champion of this sensibility than Matthieu Blazy at Chanel, whose interpretation of the wardrobe staple helped make it feel novel once again.

Of course, as temperatures drop, the natural inclination is to reach for clothing that provides maximum coverage, which often means an onslaught of pants, whether in heavy wool or denim. However, all hope is not lost for the garment of the moment as we inch towards brisker weather. The key to making it work during the colder months comes down to a thoughtful approach to layering, as well as opting for styles in substantial fabrics that can stand up to the chill.

Below, you’ll find eight skirt styles, like those in leather or denim, and how to wear them for everything from weekend adventures to boardroom meetings without feeling too buttoned-up.

Perhaps Matthieu Blazy’s design prowess could be likened to the Midas touch, because just as he has helped bring the skirt back into relevance, so too has he turned to the quarter-zip. Channel a bit of Chanel’s Métiers d’Art 2026 collection by pairing an oatmeal-colored cashmere-blend quarter-zip with a tartan skirt adorned with fringe, and introduce some daytime drama with a pair of shoulder-duster earrings. To finish things off, reach for croc-embossed Mary Jane pumps and a stark white shoulder bag for just the right amount of ladies-who-lunch panache.

Guest in Residence Tartan Wrap Fringed-Detail Skirt

Loewe Scarf Mini Knotted Leather Shoulder Bag

One of the great pleasures of fall is venturing outside the city for some much-needed fresh country air. Channel a more pastoral approach to dressing with a long, box-pleated corduroy skirt paired with a lightweight cashmere turtleneck and a plaid shirt. Top everything off with a cropped puffer jacket featuring an aviator-inspired shearling collar, a detail echoed in the retro sensibility of a pair of shield sunglasses.

Tom Ford Bronson Acetate Pilot Sunglasses

Polo Ralph Lauren Matte Woven Down Bomber Jacket

Though Western dressing seems to be an ever-present trend, there’s no denying that the aesthetic has become increasingly dominant in recent seasons. To channel it without looking like an extra in a rodeo show, the key is to mix hallmarks of the style, such as suede and turquoise accents, be it in the form of a concho belt or jewelry, with more everyday pieces. Consider a vintage-inspired gray hoodie and croc-embossed high-shaft boots that evoke the old West without feeling overwrought. Finish with a cropped bomber jacket in a similar hue to the skirt to create the impression of a matching set.

Isabel Marant Étoile Belma Faux Leather Jacket

Banana Republic x La Ligne Suede Wrap Midi Skirt

When it comes to fall dressing, it’s quite natural to gravitate toward a palette of muted tones and neutrals, perhaps punctuated by an interesting pop of red or even the color of the season, purple. However, don’t be afraid to go against the grain and embrace a more saturated palette, particularly with holiday gatherings on the horizon. Consider this hostess-ready taffeta ball skirt in a shocking pinkish-red hue, paired with a pink button-down shirt and a studded enamel starfish ring set with peridot. To temper the abundance of color, introduce touches of white in the form of a topcoat and retro-inspired kitten heels finished with a bow, lending the outfit a welcome sense of balance.

The Frankie Shop Gaia Double-Breasted Coat

Give the traditional prep-school uniform a contemporary twist by pairing an A-line denim skirt with a striped long-sleeve polo shirt and an unconventional gray wool blazer with double lapels. Instead of the typical boot, consider opting for the trendy optic-white jazz shoe for an unexpected touch. For some added visual interest and warmth, introduce a scarf in a complementary hue.

A leather skirt can, at times, feel a bit severe, so consider softening its appearance with your go-to white T-shirt and a V-neck sweater in a complementary shade of gray. For a downtown cool factor, reach for a pair of slip-on loafers in lieu of heels or a boot. Finish with a statement cuff for added polish.

Majestic Filatures Cotton Crewneck T-Shirt

Office attire has become considerably more relaxed in recent years, letting personal style shine. A utilitarian skirt turns traditional workwear on its head, but pair it with familiar staples, such as a striped monk-collar shirt and a knitted vest, both rendered in unexpected color combinations. Finish with a herringbone overcoat and second-skin wine-colored booties for a stylish ensemble that feels anything but corporate.

Jamie Haller Striped Cotton and Silk Blend Poplin Shirt

Dries Van Noten Toner Wool Contrast V-Neck Top

In recent seasons, we’ve seen a variety of animal prints come into play, well beyond the ubiquitous leopard, making room for everything from zebra and ocelot to tiger. These patterns are statement makers on their own, which is precisely why they work so well next to understated pieces. Consider styling them with a black short-sleeved cashmere T-shirt and wool cardigan, which allows the print to command attention without overwhelming. Add a waxed-cotton jacket and riding boots for an interesting counterpoint that feels both practical and playful.

#6688. Heeled Loafers Are the Shoe Trend Fashion People Are Loving Right Now

A little studious, a little unserious, and extremely easy to style.

There’s a new star shoe on the block, and it’s a little academia-core with a lot of fun. Yes, heeled loafers are the shoe fashion people are obsessing over, so you’re going to want to tap in early while you can.

We saw the shoe making its way onto several runways this past season, from Louis Vuitton and Mulberry to Diesel and Chanel. For a while now, dark academia and studious fashion have been super popular. Regular loafers are the go-to for many, whether it be wearing them to actual school or work.

The style has caught the eye of many fashion insiders, including our senior fashion editor, Jessica Neises. “The first heeled loafer I fell for this season is by Sofia Testino, an emerging NYC-based designer whose debut included the most luscious, candy-colored patent wedge loafers,” Neises says. “Other downtown brands like Vaquera have also followed suit with this shape. There’s something a little studious but still unserious about them, and the wedge is the perfect middle ground for anyone who isn’t ready to commit to a full-blown heel but doesn’t love a flat loafer either, comfortable yet still fab.”

Below, I’ve gathered some heeled loafer inspo so that you can figure out just how to style your pair.

Staying in the realm of academia, but upping the fashion, pairing your heeled loafers with a cinched blazer (and don’t forget the accessories) alongside a colorful patterned skirt is the surefire way into this trend.

As a Floridian who struggles with layering during the colder months, a good dress will always take you far. And this style works great with a more fashion-forward heeled loafer.

Similar to throwing on a dress, a good matching set will get the job done every time. Pairing a sleek leather pair of heeled loafers with this sweater set is not only stylish but super comfortable.
